* **Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes.**
* **Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details provided in the Summary Information and other relevant sections of the proposal, including the project/programme documents.**
* **The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 60. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time.**
* **In preparing the proposal, please ensure that it is consistent with the GCF’s policies and procedures as well as any other relevant guidelines or instructions provided by the Secretariat.**

# A.20. Executing Entity information

**Executing Entity (EE):** The EE is the Republic of Niger, acting through the Ministry of Agriculture with respect to channeling of GCF processes.

The Banque Ouest Africaine de Développement (BOAD) is the Accredited Entity, as well as the implementing entity for this project. BOAD has been accredited by the GCF since 2013. An incentive environment favorable to the promotion of a private and sustainable financing mechanism for agricultural research into climate change is created for the scaling up of the NHA-AJIC project.

## A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 type pages)

Niger is a landlocked, Least Developed Country (LDC) with some of the highest rates of poverty in the world. The country's economy, food security and the livelihoods of its rural communities are extremely vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. Niger has experienced an increase in temperature by 0.5°C over the past century, with average temperatures increasing and rainfall variability having severe impacts on agriculture, which is the main source of income for most households. The frequency and intensity of extreme weather events have increased; while the onset and length of the rainfall season is variable. At the same time, rainfall intensity is decreasing in some areas. In Niger have caused flooding that led to loss of life and major economic damages amounting to 15 240 615 Euros.

Food insecurity, poverty and malnutrition are increasing in Niger because of the negative impacts of climate change on the agricultural sector. The majority of Niger's population (87%) lives from agriculture, while 93% of its land is arable; 29% of people were at risk. Most agriculture in Niger is rainfed, but given the changing climate and often erratic rainfall, farmers are increasingly resorting to irrigation as a means of increasing their yields. However, this has put pressure on water resources that can be utilized sustainably for food and agricultural production.

New establishments of Agricultural Developments (Agricultures Hydropériennes - AHA) which provide irrigated areas for farmers' groups with government support. These Irrigated Agriculture Developments (AHAs) are located in the north-eastern part of Niger, where rainfall is low and erratic. The irrigation systems have a canal capacity and thus prevents irrigation water from reaching some fields. Intense rainfall events also cause soil erosion and sedimentation in canals, which reduces their efficiency over time.

Under irrigation only 698ha could be harvested, representing a loss of -20% and 102 748 Euros during the agricultural season of 2015.

The NHA-AJIC project will address these climate change problems by: (i) developing climate-resilient AHAs with solar-powered and water-efficient irrigation systems; (ii) supporting the effective operation of AHAs and building the capacity of farmers' groups to manage them sustainably. The project will be implemented in 10 new AHAs, covering a total area of 253 ha. These AHAs are located in the regions of Dosso and Zinder, which are among the most vulnerable to climate change of Niger's seven regions. In total 1000 hectares of new AHAs will be developed and 749 hectares rehabilitated. The project will also support the rehabilitation of existing irrigation infrastructure (canals, dams, temperatures, droughts, and floods). The AHAs will be cultivated by beneficiaries (women, men), youth) who will be organized in farmers' mutual production groups which will constitute a farmer's cooperative on each AHA. Solar-


---
**Page 7**
---

**GREEN CLIMATE FUND-FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE S OF 95**

power pumps will be installed at the AHAs, instead of the typically used pumps that are powered by diesel or electricity from the fossil-fuel national grid. In addition to irrigation, these pumps will also provide water for livestock and other uses. The project will also include a solar power component where appropriate, which is expected to achieve significant water savings compared to the status quo. A total of 45 MW of solar power will be installed across the project sites.

A comprehensive set of interventions will ensure the effective operation of the AHAs, namely enhancing the regulatory environment, establishing strong management structures, and building the capacity of key stakeholders such as farmers and local government officials. The project will also include a component to support the development of AHAs who will also be assisted to improve their access to markets and to undertake income generating activities such as processing of agricultural produce. Farmers’ groups, cooperatives and other important institutions will receive training in good agricultural practices, crop management and water use efficiency methods. Agricultural productivity and climate-resilience will also be enhanced by improving agro-meteorological forecasting and environmental monitoring, and by providing access to weather insurance.

Given the large adaptation needs in Niger’s agricultural sector, it is essential to scale up these climate-smart ways of farming and innovation. However, there are currently no financial instruments available for farmers or other actors involved from whom climate-smart agriculture. To overcome these challenges, the proposed GCF project will create a favorable environment for such private financing by (i) building the capacity of the private financial sector to promote and provide credit to smallholder farmers; (ii) providing guarantees to banks and other financial institutions for climate smart agriculture through local financial institutions in the form of an agricultural loan facility which fund will be provided by the GCF. The project will also include a component to support the development of a pool of regional or international development financial institutions. This proposed Agricultural Loan Facility of 5,890 million Euro will be used for financing climate-smart agriculture and adaptation activities.

This GCF project will benefit an estimated number of 121,615 people directly and one million people indirectly. The project’s direct beneficiaries will comprise: (i) farming households at the targeted Agriculture Development; (ii) people who will receive agricultural products from projects to support their food security; (iii) people who will benefit from irrigation capacity building; and (iv) people who will receive agricultural products from project sites to support their food security. The indirect beneficiaries are those who will be indirectly affected by the project, including the local communities in the project area. The estimated one million people who live in these villages will benefit from awareness-raising activities, climate-smart agriculture training and capacity building for farmers and other actors involved from whom climate-smart agriculture.

The project’s impact in terms of food security will include making available at least 5.6 M tons of cereal equivalent per year (2017-2030) to the local population, which is estimated to be able to feed an additional 48 million people per year or 227,551 CCA for 25 years, by developing solar technology instead of fossil-fuel generators and water pumps.

This project is consistent with Niger’s National Determined Contribution (NDC), particularly in terms of promoting climate-smart agriculture and considering adaptation, mitigation, and food security, while strengthening development at all levels. The project will also contribute to the achievement of several Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) priorities of the Agriculture Forestry and Other Land Use (AFOLU) sector, of water resources and capacity building of the private financial sector. The project will also contribute to the achievement of the following objectives: the results of implementation and scaling up of CSA activities, namely strengthening of assisted natural regeneration, recovery of degraded land, improvement of the cereal and forage balance and of food and nutritional security.

## B.1 Climate Change Context (1000 words, approximately two pages)

Climate change problem: Describe the climate change problem the proposal is expected to address. Describe the evidence (e.g., observed changes in temperature and precipitation patterns, extreme events, and projected impacts, exposure, and vulnerabilities) that the proposed interventions are expected to address. Also describe the most likely future conditions for the region of interest based on current scientific understanding and/or model-based interventions. Include baseline information. The methodologies used to derive such information, including the mitigation and adaptation measures.

The major portion of the Niger Basin that is outside of the Sahel zone is in the humid tropical zone of southern Niger, a region already facing high temperatures and levels of precipitation. While climate models differ, some projections suggest that this region will experience an increase in temperature and decrease in rainfall over the next century. The risk of extreme weather events such as floods and droughts. The extreme variability of the basin's climate, and the likely long-term trend toward increased aridity, are expected to continue into the future. This is consistent with the fact that the basin is unlikely. Moreover, any shift or deterioration in climate is likely to be reversed at some point in the future.

### INCREASE IN HISTORICAL AND PROJECTED TEMPERATURES

In Niger, the maximum temperature has increased by 1.7°C since the beginning of the 20th century (Figure below). From 1956-2010, the maximum temperature increased by 1.7°C and the minimum temperature by 2.4°C (Figure below). In the project area such as Tahoua Agadez and Dosso regions, the daily maximum temperatures have increased by +1.8°C (+0.3°C), +1.9°C (+0.5°C) and +1.7°C (+0.7°C) between 1960-2010. At Maima Soum (Diffa region), the daily minimum temperature has increased by +1.8°C (+0.7°C), +0.3°C (+0.8°C) and +1.5°C (+0.7°C) between 1960-2010.

The average annual temperatures in this geographical area is low (approximately 0.5°C according to the United States Geological Survey Department, 2012), the increase of temperature in the range of 0°C to +3°C will have a significant impact on the region's climate and its vulnerability to climate change. This ambient temperature is expected to increase by a minimum of 1.5°C across the regions of Diffa, Agadez and Tahoua, and 1°C across the Zinder and Tarbess regions by 2050 compared to 1980-2010 according to the RCP (Figure below).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 10 OF 95

Rainfall in Niger has decreased rapidly between 1950 and mid-1980s and partially recovered during the 1990s and 2000s.

An analysis of rainfall changes over the period 1961–2010 shows a significant decrease in rainfall since 1970 and which has been most pronounced in the north. The average annual rainfall for Niger is about 580 mm, with an interannual rainfall deficit on average around 20%, but may exceed 30% in the west and centre; a clear tendency for isolohyets to slide southward up to 1970 is observed (RDN, 2016). The rainfall distribution over the northern part of Niger and southern Sudan shows that the rainfall is relatively constant over the same. Furthermore, observations indicate a decreasing concentration of rainfall in the north and an increase in temperature at all stations, with however, a moderate increase on the Naimayi and Tahoua stations (RDN, 2016).

Between 2000 and 2009, the average rainfall in Niger's crop growing districts was about 8 percent lower than the 1920–
69 period. The decrease is most pronounced over the northern part of the country where the average annual rainfall has decreased by more than 30%. In addition, the northern tropical Atlantic has increased in temperature over this period; this has drawn the summer rains further north, increasing the aridity and desertification of the Sahel region (RDN, 2016). The decrease in rainfall is expected to continue, resulting in inadequate rainfall for viable agricultural livelihoods, as maps of anticipated changes in rainfall, and as time series plots (Figure 8).

![Figure 8: Projected mean annual rainfall increases across Niger over the period 2021-2040 compared to a baseline period of 1961 to 2013 under the SSP3.4 scenario](image_url)

On average, Niger's climate is used to alternate between wet and dry periods every few years, during the period 1960-87. Since then, however, there has been a new mode of rainfall variability characterized by a succession of wet and dry years. This pattern appears to have continued in recent years under the effect of climate change (Figure 10a). The increase in mean annual rainfall over time is also reflected in the fact that areas with a long-term mean annual rainfall for a given area, have migrated to the south in Niger as the country has become drier. The 600 mm sub-humid zone has shifted south by about 25 km since the beginning of the century and now covers an area of more than 100,000 km² over two decades. The 800 mm isohyet completely disappeared from the Niger around 1995, i.e., nowhere in Niger receives this amount of rainfall today (Figure 10b). In addition to the decrease in mean annual rainfall, extreme wet and dry events have increased over the period 1960-2010 and are predicted to continue increasing for the period 2020-2050. These changes confirm the increasing trend of rainfall extremes across the project area.

## ADAPTATION NEEDS

Climate change, which can be translated in the project intervention area by an increase of temperatures, the increase of dry spells and heavy rainfalls and variable trend of the onset and length of the rainy season, is affecting soil, and agricultural productivity.

In Niger, the various types of soils are subject to degradation due to the adverse effects related to the rising temperatures and the hydrological character of the rainy season. The most visible soil degradation are, among others: (i) the progressive reduction in the thickness of the topsoil layer; (ii) the increase in the proportion of clayey particles on the surface following the drying up of the topsoil layers and their infiltration by the precipitation of iron oxides and aluminum hydroxides; (iii) the decrease in the organic matter content due to the loss of humus, erosion and salinization and (iv) the alteration of the balance of soil ecosystems. These other climates induce silting of shallow lands and the formation of gullies on steep slopes.

The project area is characterized by a high level of vulnerability to climate change and degradation, and conservation actions for maintaining soil quality and being able to practice efficient irrigation.

Broadly, the human pressure on land results from the expansion of crop fields in Niger. In 1960, the rural population constituted about 75% of the total population; this proportion has decreased to 42%, 38%, 18% and 24.5% respectively in 1975, 2000 and 2013. Particularly in Zinder and Maradi, the land already cultivated in 1975 is now a well-established agricultural area.

The expansion of crop fields has led to the degradation of soils and the transformation of savannas into croplands. In order to combat land degradation due to extensive agriculture which uses unprotected extended areas most of the time, the project proposes to implement the farmer-managed natural regeneration technique (FMNR) where farmers have experimented successfully with the farmer-managed natural regeneration technique™ which is currently promoted by the International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN). The choice of the conservation and degradation strategies was focused on the implementation of sustainable land management practices on land identified as degraded.

Strong greening trends were reported in Tchad but some studies have even estimated at 100% the increase in the number of trees per hectare. However, this greening is not necessarily due to more trees planted in the landscape and according to some reports, this greening does not necessarily mean more trees planted in the landscape but rather a consequence of the cropping systems intercalibration by farmers during the last two decades as reported in 2018.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 14 OF 95

A response to increasing population pressure. In other areas of the region, farmers have planted, protected and managed trees to increase woody cover as a response to the droughts of the 1970s and 1980s [1]. Based on these experiences, our proposal is to plant trees in Niger (see Figure G) with the climate resilient practices that have been experimented with success for reducing human population pressure and increasing food security.

Agriculture, the main activity of the Niger's rural population is essentially rain-fed and is therefore highly vulnerable to climate change impacts. The potential impacts of climate change linked to increased temperatures, increasing dry spells and more frequent extreme weather events are expected to have a significant impact on crop yields (Figure 13). These impacts include:
(i) water shortage to meet crop water requirements during crop cycles;
(ii) outbreaks of pests such as leaf miner caterpillars of the cotton bollworm (Helicoverpa armigera) and fall army worm (Spodoptera frugiperda);
(iii) increased incidence of plant diseases.

In terms of pests, leaf miner caterpillar outbreaks are expected to become more frequent with the increasing length of dry spells. The frequency and intensity of cotton bollworm and fall army worm outbreaks is also expected to increase in higher temperatures as reported by farmers [2]. These insect infestations are also expected to increase in higher temperatures as reported by farmers [2]. Restoring faster insect development shorter life cycle, increased reproduction rate and greater survival rates of the insects are expected.

The potential impacts on crop yields of climate change are illustrated in Figure 13. The figure shows that the yield of cotton is projected to decrease under current climate conditions (a) and with a 1°C increase in temperature and migration patterns (b). A 2°C increase in temperature and migration patterns would result in a further reduction in cotton yields.

Many studies are consistent with predicting yield reductions in the near future under the current changing climate. Modelled yield reductions for Niger range from less than 10% if the case of a 2°C increase in temperature and migration patterns to nearly 50%. An increase of 3°C will lead to a decrease in agricultural yields of around 15 to 25% [Sart et al., 2007; Hillel, 2008]. The yield reduction is expected to be more pronounced for maize (4.6%) and sorghum (9.3%) than in Marchand region and by 5.5% in Zinder region. The map on the right-hand side (Figure 13c) shows the potential reduction of cotton yields under current climate conditions, assuming irrigation is applied on present day harvested areas with climate change, assuming irrigation water is not limiting.

Achieving food security goals in West Africa, depends on the capacity of the agricultural sector to feed the rapidly growing population. The region has experienced a dramatic increase in its population over the past 50 years. In Niger, the cereal surplus, which covered 86% of food needs until the early 1970s, has become structurally in deficit since the end of the 1970s [3]. This structural deficit is expected to continue and will increase further as a result of higher food consumption. Severe food insecurity persists and is affecting an increasing number of households. From 2015 to 2018, the proportion of Nigeriens living in poverty more than doubled: from 17% in 2015 to 32%.

**CLIMATE CHANGE AND FOOD INSECURITY**

As mentioned above, the adverse effects of climate have extremely negative impacts on production and production factors. In addition to this is the high cost of energy. All this contributes to food insecurity and reduces farmers' incomes. For example, in Niger, the costs associated with irrigation are very high: 100 kg of rice requires 250 liters of water; the setting up of irrigation canals and the drying up of cultivated plots, 716 kg of rice were lost during the course of one season with a loss of income of 803.5 tons of rice (i.e., 2 704 728 FCFA).

Indeed, the various surveys report on the vulnerability to household food insecurity in Niger confirm the worsening of the

However, the various forecasts indicate that people's vulnerability to climate change will continue to increase in Niger, with the consequent growing food insecurity.

According to the NAPA, the potential impacts of climate change related to a situation of decreasing rainfall and increasing temperatures could be: (i) an extension of the dry season; (ii) a reduction in the length of the rainy season; (iii) an increase in the frequency of dry days during the agricultural season; (iv) insufficient water availability for crops needs; (v) the appearance of new pests.

To illustrate this situation, three studies were conducted: one with a plant growth model, the other two on a purely statistical basis, to assess the impacts of climate change on the development and production of millet in Niger.

The first study (Sakack et al. 2008) used historical data covering the period 1961 - 1999 from 12 synoptic stations in the Sudano-Sahelian zone of Niger:

| Figure 13 | Changes in mean growth cycle under different climate scenarios |
| --- | --- |
| **Source:** Sakack et al. 2008 |

The results obtained on the basis of a simulation of the current growth cycle by 2 to 3 days per decade until 2050 and by 4 to 5 days by 2050. Figure 11 shows that this change will be observed at the local level for one entire season, i.e., from 92 days.

The second study, based on purely statistical considerations, concludes that there will be a 37% drop in millet yields by 2050 compared to the average yield of the period June-August-September. It should be recalled that rainfall is well correlated with maximum temperatures of the same period, which could have negative impacts on rainfed millet cultivation.

The third study (Sakack et al., 2013) was based on a logistic regression model, up to 42% of the variability of millet yield in Niger can be attributed to rainfall. The results obtained here confirm well the conclusions of the Ken IPCC Report concerning a decrease in cereal crop yields because of rising temperatures in tropical regions.

Niger is promoting irrigated agriculture through small-scale irrigation and irrigated Agriculture for Food Security and Sustainable Development (AIFSSD) projects.

However, the AHAAs implemented so far are subject to climatic, technical, and financial constraints, which severely limit their operational performance. In areas where the AHAAs are located in Niger (Maradi, Zinder, Dosso, Niamey), under a highly rainy season, the increasing occurrence of heavy rainfall events is exacerbating soil erosion and is inducing the sedimentation of irrigation canals and channels (see Figure 9). During such events, strong runoff transports sand and sediments into the irrigation and drainage channels, which reduces their capacity to convey water. This phenomenon is particularly pronounced in the rainy season (Figure 10 and 12). During dry spells exacerbated by heat waves, the direction of irrigation channels reduces the available water for crops, while the sedimentation of canals and channels increases the risk of flooding. The problem is further exacerbated by poor design, lack of maintenance, etc., which rapidly weakens the hydro-agricultural infrastructure and considerably limits its operational performance (see Table 1 for the years 2011 to 2015).

For example, in 2015, more than 1.996 ha of hydro-agricultural infrastructures land were exposed to long dry spells and floods.

### Understanding of the causes of the decline in agricultural yields

While the Government estimates that harvests were good in the 2016 agricultural season, the household food security survey conducted in September and October 2016 showed that 43.4 percent of producers reported that the 2016 harvest was worse than that of 2015. The percentage of producers reporting a better harvest (37.8%) is lower compared to what was higher than that of 2017.

The project is consistent with Nigeria's National Determined Contribution (NDC), particularly on promoting climate-smart agriculture (CSA) and considering adaptation, mitigation, and food security, while strengthening development at the grassroots level. The NDC also emphasizes the need to promote sustainable land management practices in the sector (mitigation), improving agricultural productivity and income (production). Indeed, the development of hydro- electric power generation, in line with the complementary actions of adaptation and mitigation advocated in the NDC. The project will also contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) by promoting sustainable use of natural resources and capacity building of the actors at all levels identified in the NDC; the related benefits of the AFGLU sector, including the promotion of renewable energy sources such as solar power generation, the conservation of natural regeneration (RNA), recovery of degraded land, improvement of the cereal and forage balance and of food and nutritional security, etc.

### References:

* **Action Project for Climate Resilience in Niger:** Also describes any record to ongoing initiatives/programmes that are linked to the project from other domestic or international sources of funding, such as the Global Environment Facility, Adaptation Fund, Climate Investment Funds, etc. The Action Project for Climate Resilience in Niger is a multi-donor initiative.
* **Please identify current gaps and barriers regarding recent or ongoing projects and elaborate further how this project will address them:**

Currently, three major agriculture/program projects are underway in Niger to strengthen the resilience of farming populations to climate change for ensuring food security. These are: (1) The Community Action Project for Climate Resilience (PROMARIVE), which is a multi-donor initiative that aims at strengthening the capacity of smallholder irrigation systems to climate change to increase national food security. Unlike the proposed project which covers most of Niger, the PROMARIVE project focuses on the northern part of the country and mainly targets the rehabilitation of existing irrigation schemes and improving grass area production. In addition to the mainstreaming of climate change into health, water and road infrastructure projects, the project also aims at strengthening the capacity of smallholder farmers through the rehabilitation of socio-economic community facilities prone to climate risks; (2) the Mobilization and Valorization of Water Resources (MIVAR), which is a multi-donor initiative that aims at improving access to water resources for rural populations character which is under implementation in the northern parts of the Tchadian, Dosso, Tahoua and Agadez regions. Thus, the project will not only contribute to the achievement of the SDGs but also complement the objectives of this proposed project; Even in Tahoua and Agadez, the intervention sites of PROMARIVE are different from the proposed GCP project site. The MIVAR project is a multi-donor initiative that aims at improving access to water resources for rural populations through the mobilization of water resources for irrigation and the popularization of resistant seeds and improved irrigation techniques. For the mobilization of water resources, PROMARIVE is also supported in the development of small-scale irrigation (the

### Project for maladaptation

Project Description

The climate smart agriculture approach that has been selected by the project includes measures that are "likely no-regret" as they will be beneficial even in a scenario of low climate change impacts, and are likely to have co-benefits. Furthermore, the project proposes climate smart agriculture interventions have been designed, and will be implemented, based on the results of an impact assessment conducted by the project team.

The project's climate smart agriculture measures target the most vulnerable smallholder farmers in rural Niger. So, it is not anticipated that the measures will result in the unsustainable expansion of agriculture in the target areas once the benefits have been exhausted. The project will also provide training on climate change adaptation techniques and best practices with commercialization, ensuring that land cultivation is carefully controlled and that benefit sharing amongst vulnerable smallholder farmers is ensured.

It should be noted that the project's measures are unlikely to result in the expansion of agriculture into cleared land or increase the size of farms, which would result in the degradation of ecosystems and the overexploitation of natural resources. With the decrease of the rainfall, the increase of the food insecurity, there is a need to increase the irrigation area in Niger to achieve food security; water is needed to develop small-scale irrigation and hydro-agricultural infrastructures. ### B.2. Theory of change (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagram)

The theory of change is a logical sequence of events that describes how the project development pathway towards a low-emission and/or climate-resilient direction. Provide the diagram of the theory of change (approximately 1 page).

**Problem statement and proposed solution**

Niger is the poorest of the Least Developed Countries constituency (LDC) in the world* and faces extraordinary challenges to its economic, social and environmental development. The country’s economy is heavily dependent on agriculture which is the main source of income and livelihood provision for 87% of the population, yet it is severely impacted by the adverse effects of climate change. Niger has experienced a significant increase in temperature over recent years, leading to more frequent crop failures during the rainy season. Notably, the increasing intensity and frequency of floods and droughts under climate change are expected to continue to negatively impact agricultural productivity. The country’s soils have lost their functionality and potential to increase agricultural production. Flooding damages fields, increases erosion and causes salinization. Droughts reduce soil moisture content and decrease crop yields. These factors further reduce agricultural productivity further. As a result, food insecurity, poverty and malnutrition are increasing and have led to 29% of the population living below the poverty line.

To remedy this, the preferred solution is the deployment and rehabilitation of AHA’s that are climate resilient which is crucial to help communities adapt to climate change, as well as decrease the amount of GHG emissions currently produced through agricultural activities. The project will be implemented in 10 LGAs (Local Government Areas) across Niger. The project aims to enable the development of a replicable model of climate-resilient, low-emission agriculture financing with the objective of increasing agricultural productivity and income for smallholder farmers. This will contribute towards the region will increase agricultural productivity and income as crop production will yield more and communities will be able to turn their attention from food security to other economic activities.

The project zone is one of the most vulnerable regions and communities in Niger’s Tahoua, Agadez, Maradi, Diffa and Zinder regions. It calls for a total of 10,000 ha of AHA’s be developed and 748 ha of old AHA’s to be rehabilitated across the region. The project will also provide water supply infrastructure in the form of boreholes, wells, dams, canals, irrigation and flood drought events. This will be complemented by the installation of solar-powered pumps at AHA’s, replacing those that are currently powered by diesel generators. The project will also include a climate-resilient agriculture component which increased water supplies for human-and livestock-consumption as well as reduced amounts of money spent on fuel and other inputs. The project will also provide training to farmers in the use of climate-resilient practices to increase the climate resilience of 121,615 direct beneficiaries and 1,000,000 indirect beneficiaries, while also helping communities to be low-carbon development pathways with a 3,171.63 CO2e avoided.

The programme has a strong focus on gender equality, youth empowerment, climate-resilient agricultural technologies, cost-effective production methods, public and private funding of the AHA’s and the inclusion of local women and vulnerable people as all are welcome. Niger signatures is vulnerable to the impacts of climate change.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 23 OF 95

## Goal Statement

If climate-resilient irrigated agricultural systems and strengthened capacity for their management result in increased productivity, food security, and resilience to climate change (variable rainfall, droughts and floods) of Niger's most vulnerable communities will be enhanced because irrigation systems are improved, sustainably maintained and well managed. The project will contribute to the achievement of Sustainable Development Goal 2 (SDG-2) by promoting small-scale smart agriculture, and support from the private sector; GCF grant financing will therefore facilitate the update of the irrigated agricultural management model from a conventional-agriculture technology and creating a paradigm shift toward low emission and climate-resilient development.

### Project Proposal

The proposed project's goal will be achieved through the realization of three outcomes and their underlying results:

**Outcome 1:** Improved water use, energy efficiency of reduced GHG emissions from irrigation and increased resilience to flooding of irrigated agricultural systems in Niger. This outcome is realized by Component 2 (sustainable development and rehabilitation of perimeters vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change), which is a combination of Results 3.1 – Knowledge practices and regulations for irrigated agriculture; Result 3.2 – Technical linearities adapted to major climatic risks of the irrigated perimeters are adopted for crop production; Result 4.1 – Funding and market access mechanisms for products from irrigated perimeters are established in the perimeters, and Result 2.4 – Funding and market access mechanisms for products from irrigated perimeters are established.

**Outcome 2:** Increased agricultural productivity and food security under climate change conditions. Outcome 2 will be realized through Component 2 (increased agricultural productivity and food security under climate change conditions), which is a combination of Results 3.1 – Knowledge practices and regulations for irrigated agriculture; Result 3.2 – Technical linearities adapted to major climatic risks of the irrigated perimeters are adopted for crop production, Result 4.1 – Funding and market access mechanisms for products from irrigated perimeters are established in the perimeters, and Result 2.4 – Funding and market access mechanisms for products from irrigated perimeters are established.

**Outcome 3:** Strengthened capacity (technical organizational and financial) for climate smart agriculture. Outcome 3 will be realized through Component 1 (strengthening of technical capacity of local producers and actors to implement climate-resilient agricultural practices), which is made up of: Result 3.1 – Knowledge, practices and regulations for irrigated agriculture; Result 3.2 – Technical linearities adapted to major climatic risks of the irrigated perimeters are adopted for crop production; Result 4.1 – Funding and market access mechanisms for products from irrigated perimeters are established in the perimeters, and Result 4.2 – Funding and market access mechanisms for products from irrigated perimeters are established.

### Barriers and Risks to the Realization of Project Outcomes and Approaches to Overcoming them

**Barrier 1:** Limited technical capacity and knowledge of government staff and local stakeholders (farmers, cooperatives and other organizations) in implementing climate-resilient agricultural practices. This barrier will be addressed by strengthening the technical capacity of local producers and actors to implement climate-resilient agricultural practices.

**Barrier 2:** Poorly maintained and damaged (from floods) existing AHAs are reducing the climate resilience of agriculture production systems resulting in reduced agricultural productivity and contributing to food insecurity. Barrier 2 will be addressed through strengthening the technical capacity of local producers and actors to implement climate-resilient agricultural practices, Result 3.1 – Knowledge, practices and regulations for irrigated agriculture; Result 3.2 – Technical linearities adapted to major climatic risks of the irrigated perimeters are adopted for crop production, and Result 4.1 – Funding and market access mechanisms for products from irrigated perimeters are established in the perimeters.

**Barrier 3:** Limited financial capacity of farmers' groups for the implementation of climate-resilient actions (Result 3.2). These results contribute to establishing funding and market access mechanisms for products from irrigated perimeters, Result 4.1 – Funding and market access mechanisms for products from irrigated perimeters are established in the perimeters.

**Barrier 4:** Limited financial capacity of farmers' groups for the implementation of climate-resilient actions (Result 3.2), which ultimately results in the realization of strengthened capacity (technical, organizational and financial) for climate smart agriculture. This barrier will be addressed by strengthening the technical capacity of local producers and actors to implement climate-resilient agricultural practices, Result 3.1 – Knowledge, practices and regulations for irrigated agriculture; Result 3.2 – Technical linearities adapted to major climatic risks of the irrigated perimeters are adopted for crop production, and Result 4.1 – Funding and market access mechanisms for products from irrigated perimeters are established in the perimeters.

Barrier 2: Lack of organization capital within farmers' groups and cooperatives to coordinate the collaboration between producers and agro-input suppliers, as well as to implement climate-resilient agricultural technologies practices. Barrier 4: Limited availability of funding options coupled with high interest rates charged by banks on lending products needed to introduce, implement and maintain climate-resilient agricultural technologies and practices in Niger—as such, it is necessary to address the lack of financing options through the promotion and scaling of innovative financing mechanisms with attractive interest rates. This will be addressed through facilitating the promotion and scaling of innovative financing and on-lending mechanisms with attractive interest rates for the implementation of climate-resilient agricultural technologies and practices (Outcome 2). | **Ris.5:** Agricultural plots (AHAAs and their perimeters) are damaged by climate change-induced floods, resulting in erosion and restricting agricultural productivity and food security gains – i.e., reduced functionality of the AHAAs. Risk is will be addressed as follows: a) The construction of flood protection structures such as embankments, dikes, check dams and terraces, building anti-erosion structures, flood perimeter protection strengthening, anti-erosive control by planting trees around plots (Activity 2.1.5), and b) the development of water harvesting techniques (Activity 3.3.4). These activities will contribute to support for sustainable development of agricultural land and its rehabilitation (Component 2), which will lead to an increase in agricultural productivity and food security under climate change conditions (Outcome 2). Result 3.2 will contribute to developing capacities (technical, organizational and financial) for climate smart agriculture in Niger (Outcome 5). |
| **Ris.6:** Water resources required for improvements to agricultural productivity and food security are insufficient or are overexploited under conditions of climate change resulting disrupted supplies. Risk is will be addressed through the development of water harvesting techniques (Activity 3.3.4) and by strengthening the regulation of water use (to prevent overexploitation). Innovative irrigation techniques will include drip and sprinkler irrigation, which will contribute to support for sustainable development of agricultural land and its rehabilitation (Component 2), which will lead to an increase in agricultural productivity and food security under climate change conditions (Outcome 2). It is expected that, through the project's water-saving interventions, 18.6 million m³ of water per year will be saved – Activities 3.3.4 and 3.3.5. These activities will contribute to support for sustainable development of agricultural land and its rehabilitation (Component 2), which will lead to an increase in agricultural productivity and food security under climate change conditions (Outcome 2). Result 3.2 will contribute to developing capacities (technical, organizational and financial) for climate smart agriculture in Niger (Outcome 5). |

## Water-saving technologies and clean energy systems (Result 1.2). Results 1.1 and 1.2 will, together, contribute to sustainable development and rehabilitation of perimeters vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change (Component 1).

### Activities
- **Activity 1.2.1:** Establishing water-saving technologies and clean energy systems in Niger’s agricultural systems under climate change conditions (Outcome 1). Water use regulation within the project target areas will be achieved through the establishment of irrigation users associations (IUA) to manage, operate, maintain, and monitor water resources.
- **Activity 1.2.2:** Mitigating the overexploitation of groundwater — Activity 1.2.2.i: The establishment of Irrigation Users Associations (IUA) to manage, operate, maintain, and monitor water resources; ii: Establishing water-saving technologies, which reduce water usage — Activities 1.2.3 and 1.2.4; iii: Facilitating improved contracts and agreements between farmers and IUA.
- **Activity 1.2.3:** Establishing water-saving technologies (including sustainable water use) in Niger’s agricultural sector (Outcome 2). Water-saving technologies will be identified, tested, adapted, and mainstreamed into Niger's agricultural sector (including sustainable water use) — Activity 3.1.4 and iv: Strengthening the technical and organizational capacities of stakeholders to promote climate-resilient agriculture that is resilient to adverse effects of climate change.
- **Activity 1.2.4:** Organizing producers for the sustainable optimal development of developed and rehabilitated perimeters (Outcome 3). The project will support the establishment of IUA in priority areas, with a focus on water management and irrigation systems. This will prevent the overexploitation of groundwater which will be more feasible once cheaper solar-powered pumps are available.
- **Activity 1.2.5:** Strengthening the technical and organizational capacities of stakeholders to promote climate-resilient agriculture that is resilient to adverse effects of climate change (Outcome 3). The project will support the establishment of IUA in priority areas, with a focus on water management and irrigation systems.

### Results
- **Result 1.2:** Water-saving technologies and clean energy systems will be established in Niger’s agricultural sector.
- **Result 2.1:** Support for sustainable development of developed and rehabilitated perimeters (Component 2), and the strengthening of technical and organizational capacities to promote climate-resilient agriculture that is resilient to adverse effects of climate change.

### Outcomes
- **Outcome 3:** The project will contribute to the achievement of Niger’s national objectives to eradicaté famine, droughts and floods, as well as decreased reliance on fossil fuels in the country's agricultural sector.
- **Outcome 2:** Water-saving technologies and clean energy systems will be established in Niger’s agricultural sector.

## Assumptions
Key assumptions that apply to the proposed project's outcomes and their underlying activities are listed below:
- Local communities, existing institutions and organizations actively take up and implement the climate-smart agricultural techniques introduced by the project.
- Local communities, existing institutions and organizations are committed to the transition towards fossil-fuel-generated energy to clean energy.
- Women and youth from the project’s target communities actively participate in project activities that strengthen water management and irrigation systems.
- Current private sector stakeholders have buy-in for the move towards a climate-smart low-emissions approach in Niger.
- Local actors and producers in Niger's agricultural sector buy into and participate in climate smart agriculture projects.
- Private financial sector stakeholders commit to the promotion of innovative financing mechanisms for climate smart agriculture in Niger.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 27 OF 95

## OVERVIEW

### Objectives
- **Objective 1:** Increased water use efficiency in agriculture and improved livelihoods of smallholder farmers through the adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices.
- **Objective 2:** Increased agricultural productivity, food security, and resilience to climate change.
- **Objective 3:** Development capacity (technical and institutional) for sustainable management of natural resources.

### Results
- **Result 1.1:** Farmers are empowered with knowledge on the adoption of climate-smart agriculture practices and technologies.
- **Result 1.2:** Technological adaptation by farmers to reduce water use in agriculture.
- **Result 1.3:** Improved environmental conditions for sustainable agricultural production.

### Outputs
- **Component 1:** Development and dissemination of climate-smart agricultural practices and technologies.
- **Component 2:** Support for sustainable communities through the adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices.
- **Component 3:** Developing technical and institutional capacity for sustainable management of natural resources.

## PROJECT ACTIVITIES

### Risks
- **Risk 1.1:** Farmers groups will not be able to support themselves financially due to lack of income from their activities.
- **Risk 1.2:** Water resources may be insufficient or contaminated, leading to reduced agricultural productivity.
- **Risk 1.3:** Water prices are higher than expected, reducing the profitability of water use in agriculture.

### Barriers
- **Barrier 1:** Limited technical capacity and knowledge for sustainable management practices.
- **Barrier 2:** Lack of financial resources for the implementation of climate-smart agricultural practices.
- **Barrier 3:** High costs associated with the adoption of new technologies and practices.

## IMPLEMENTATION

### Local communities currently rely on a still easy from fossil fuel generated water to clean energy.
- Current private sector stakeholders have buy-in for the transition towards a climate-smart low emissions approach in the agriculture sector.
- Private financial sector stakeholders are not yet prepared or motivated financing mechanisms for climate-resilient agriculture.

# B.3 Project/Programme description (max. 2000 words, approximately 4 pages)

## Objective of the project

The overall objective of the project is to contribute to the increase in national agricultural production by strengthening the resilience of irrigation areas vulnerable to adverse effects of climate change.

### Specific Objectives
- **Component 1: Sustainable development and rehabilitation of irrigation areas vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change**
    - Develop irrigation areas with techniques resilient to the adverse effects of climate change.
    - Ensure the sustainability of operations, infrastructure, and their funding through a participatory approach involving stakeholders at all levels.

### Project Structure
The project is structured around three components:
1. **Component 1: Sustainable development and rehabilitation of irrigation areas vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change**
2. **Component 2: Climate-resilient agricultural production systems**
3. **Component 3: Stakeholders' technical, organizational, and financial capacities development for the climate-resilient agricultural practices**

## Component 1: Sustainable development and rehabilitation of irrigation areas vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change and reducing GHG emissions

### Output 1.1: Irrigation areas are developed with techniques resilient to the adverse effects of climate change
#### Direct beneficiaries:
- **121,615 Farmers** (Producers organized or willing to be organized as cooperatives or associations) in the project's target area.
- **Farmers** (Producers organized or willing to be organized as cooperatives or associations) in the project's target area.

### Activities
#### Activity 1.1.1: Development of irrigation areas with climate-resilient techniques
- Develop irrigation areas using climate-resilient, low-cost agriculture technologies.
- Ensure that the project will be used for the following:
    - Developing irrigation areas with climate-resilient techniques (Activity 1.1.1).
    - Rehabilitation of 749 hectares of the old AHAN (Activity 1.1.2).

#### Activity 1.1.2: Rehabilitation of 749 hectares of the old AHAN
- Develop and rehabilitate irrigation areas with climate-resilient techniques.
- Consider two activities:
    - Development of irrigation areas with climate-resilient techniques (Activity 1.1.1).
    - Rehabilitation of 749 hectares of the old AHAN (Activity 1.1.2).

### Eligibility Criteria for relevant outputs and activities under Component 1
#### Activities for which beneficiaries will be eligible:
- Irrigation areas developed with techniques resilient to the adverse effects of climate change.
- Irrigation areas and local communities in the project's target area through participatory approach involving GGOHANA, cooperatives, or associations.

### Potential selection processes
- Selection process: Through participatory approach involving GGOHANA, cooperatives, or associations.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 29 OF 95

## Activity 1.1.1
This activity will include the climate-resilient development of 1,000 hectares of irrigation areas operated by cooperatives and farmers' associations. The water resources will be sublimated into Irrigated Agricultural Developments (AHAks), each of which will be cultivated by a farmer's cooperative. AHAks will be between 10 and 71.7 ha. Each AHAk will firstly be subdivided into 5 hydraulic quarters to be developed in the irrigation areas, serving as a basic parameter for determining the amount of work to be carried out. The first quarter is typically developed within one year after the start of the project. The second quarter is typically developed during the second year and so on.

## Activity 1.1.2
This will be old AHAks at the Djebel El Gaine site that will be rehabilitated through this activity. The functionality of these AHAks has been frequently reduced as a result of damage caused by flooding and erosion. This has resulted in increased production costs and costs required to rebuild and maintain them. Therefore, the rehabilitation measures to be implemented under Activity 1.1.2 are: i) constructing anti-erosion structures; ii) installing water-saving techniques (e.g., drip irrigation systems); iii) replacing 65 km of irrigation canals with buried PVC piping; iv) rehabilitating 18 km of drains by re-profiling and curing them; v) rehabilitating 9 km of access roads to the AHAks.

## Figure 26: Division of Irrigated Agricultural Developments (AHAks) into hydraulic quarters and individual farming plots.
Each irrigated agricultural development, i.e., developments using irrigation water from rivers or groundwater, is divided into four hydraulic quarters. Each quarter has a set of planting panels and a pump.

| Hydraulic Quarter | Each hydraulic quarter is made up of a set of planting panels and a pump |
| --- | --- |

## Output 1.2
The irrigation areas are developed with water-saving techniques and a clean energy system for Euros that are relatively low in value near River Niger. These waters are in fact subject to high evaporation and are widely used for both agricultural purposes (irrigation) and domestic use. The main problem is the lack of sufficient groundwater resources, limiting the supply of water necessary for the development of crops. This project intends to use relatively abundant groundwater resources by developing irrigation areas with drip and California irrigation systems. These systems have irrigation yields of 90% and 85%, respectively, and are expected to significantly increase crop production in the area. The irrigation areas will be equipped with solar pumps and installation of drip and California irrigation systems. The steps to implementing Activity 1.2.1 is described below.

## Drill and install 688 boreholes and water storage basins.
* Install in perennials per 5 ha hydraulic quarters.
* Install drip irrigation units across 5% of the 5 ha hydraulic quarters.
* Install solar-powered pumps for irrigation across 10% of the 5 ha hydraulic quarters.
* Construct five 10 m³ water storage reservoirs per 5 ha hydraulic quarter.

This component aims at increasing the capacity for the development and rehabilitation works to support the sustainable increase in and maintenance of agricultural productivity and income of farmers' groups and cooperatives in irrigated areas through the following outputs:

Output 2.2: Technical itineraries adapted to the major climatic risks of irrigated areas.
* These will be developed and tested in pilot sites, with a view to their operational use for optimal development of irrigation areas.
* Output 2.4: Funding and market access mechanisms for products from Component 1.

## Component 3: Strengthening technical, organizational, and financial capacities for development of climate-resilient agricultural practices promotion

The objective is to strengthen the knowledge, skills, and practices of farmers and producers on techniques that are resilient to the adverse effects of climate change to facilitate project implementation, ownership, and sustainability. This component will be in place a year after project start-up and will last 3 years.

### Output 3.1: Strengthening local technical capacities for promoting agricultural support services

The objective is to strengthen the knowledge, skills, and practices of farmers and producers on techniques that are resilient to the adverse effects of climate change through the scaling up of the AHA-ARC project. Five results are expected from the implementation of this output:

1. The capacity of local service providers to provide agricultural support services in the project area is strengthened.
2. The skills of climate smart agriculture are strengthened - Output 3.2: The technical and organizational capacities of farmers' groups and cooperatives are strengthened for the adoption of climate change-resistant actions. Output 3.3: An innovative approach to the development of a private and public sector financing mechanism for promoting climate change is created for the replication of the AHA-ARC project; Output 3.4: Control, supervision, and M&E of project activities; Output 3.5: Strengthening local capacities for engaging with stakeholders and determining an overall strengthening of the agriculture sector facing climate change effect.

## Outcome 3.2: The technical and organizational capacities of farmers' groups and cooperatives are strengthened for the implementation of climate-resilient actions

For each partner, 25 farmers representing their farmers’ groups and cooperatives will be trained. At least 625 farmers (group members) will benefit from these trainings.

### Activities:

1. **Activity 3.2.1: Training on Climate-Resilient Actions**
   - The training will cover the following topics:
     - Crop intercropping
     - Water management and water harvesting techniques
     - Fertilizer application methods
     - Gender equality in agriculture

2. **Activity 3.2.2: Strengthening Organizational Capacities of Farmers’ Groups and Cooperatives**
   - The project will strengthen the organizational capacities of farmers' groups and cooperatives to implement climate-resilient actions.
   - This includes:
     - Access to information on early warning systems for water scarcity
     - Access to information on sustainable land management practices

3. **Activity 3.2.3: Creating a Sustainable Financial Mechanism**
   - The project will create a financial mechanism for the ARA-ACA project, using funds from GCF.
   - This will help in:
     - Financing climate-resilient agricultural activities
     - Access to information on early warning systems and sustainable land management practices

### Benefits:

1. **Improved Agricultural Practices**
2. **Enhanced Productivity**
3. **Reduced Risk of Agricultural Investments**

### Challenges:

- Lack of technical, financial, and other means.
- Limited access to climate-resilient agricultural techniques across the project area.

### Proposed Actions:

The proposed GCF project will ensure that farmers have access to information on early warning systems for water scarcity and sustainable land management practices. This will help them implement climate-resilient actions effectively.

The challenge is to adopt at the national level the climate smart agriculture approach that will be implied by your project in specific areas. If food insecurity is to be tackled in a sustainable manner.

In Niger's current economic context, characterized by high population growth and the prospect of progressive and rapid urbanization, it is estimated that the demand for food products will have to increase significantly. The World Bank analyzes that this market will continue to grow until 2050. Over the period 2018-2030, the average growth rate of agricultural production in Niger is expected to be around 4%. Consequently, the quantitative and qualitative improvement of the supply of agro-products and its adaptation to demand are essential for ensuring food security.

The project will contribute to this objective by implementing a series of activities that will lead to: (i) an increase in the availability of irrigated land; (ii) an increase in yields per hectare obtained in irrigation systems, including an available amount of irrigable land (270,000 ha), the availability of an abundant reserve of renewable water resources (1.5 billion m3/year); and (iii) a reduction in the cost of production thanks to the use of modern techniques for efficient mobilization of irrigation water using a clean energy source, and the increase in yields per hectare obtained in irrigation systems.

The project will be implemented by the present ANACI project, the willingness of former cooperatives to organize themselves, manifested through their participation in the project, and the commitment of the Ministry of Agriculture as an example of a long national experience in agricultural development and financing provided by a public institution such as ONAH, and the private sector.

This is clearly an opportunity for the sustainable financing of Niger's agriculture through its private financial sector by the creation of a loan facility. The project will therefore contribute to the establishment of a revolving fund that local financial institutions can therefore meet these needs if the issue of financing their own agricultural activities at incentive rates is resolved. In this series, it is necessary to scale up the impact of the grant-financed project activities and to ensure their long-term sustainability.

The proposed GCF project will contribute to the establishment of a revolving fund that local financial institutions can complement and replace the current public financing provided by the State of Niger, the BOAD and the GCF in the form of grants.

This component of the proposed GCF project will create an enabling environment to prepare stakeholders for the promotion of climate smart agriculture through the private sector. The objective is to establish a revolving fund that local financial institutions can use to directly support the financing of the sale of agricultural products and services. The activities planned under this Output are: Activity 3.3.1 Building the capacity of the private financial sector; Activity 3.3.2 Set up an attractive on-lending mechanism for climate smart agriculture through local financial institutions in the form of a revolving fund (the "Agricultural Loan Facility"); and Activity 3.3.3 Establishing a revolving fund with regional or international development financial institutions (the "Agricultural Loan Facility"). The GCF will provide funding to ONAH to support the establishment of the revolving fund, and BOAD to put in place an Agricultural Loan Facility, in accordance with its terms and conditions to be agreed with the GCF.

Activity 3.3.1: Building the capacity of the private financial sector, farmers' groups, associations and cooperatives to finance climate smart agriculture

This activity will be conducted to facilitate the setting up of an attractive on-lending mechanism for climate smart agriculture through local financial institutions in the form of an agricultural loan facility with funding from GCF, which could be scaled up by other public or private actors. The objective is to establish a revolving fund that local financial institutions can use to directly support the financing of the sale of agricultural products and services.

With the sub-activity 3.3.1.3, Assistance to build operational and institutional capacities of Financial Institutions (FI), the GCF will provide funding for: (i) the preparation of an execution plan; and prior to the disbursement of funds for Output 3.3 of the Funded Activity, the accredited entity shall finalize and submit to the Fund an additional report, in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat.

- assesses the financial viability of the Agricultural Loan Facility, including consideration of likely default rates; and
- proposes a design for the Facility that can maximize potential both to cover in private finance and to help vulnerable farmers.

If the market study confirms the potential for the loan facility, then prior to the disbursement of funds for Output 3.3 of the Funded Activity, the AE should provide, in form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat, a detailed technical assistance report on the design of the Facility.

The sub-Activity 3.3.1 will complete the sub-activity 3.3.1.3 through a training workshop for the establishment of an enabling environment for more massive climate-smart agriculture funding proposed for the benefit of Niger's private financial sector.

### Criteria for Selection of Banks and Micro finance institutions which will benefit from the agricultural loan facility

#### Items
- **Governance, Risk management rules**
  - Legally registered as a financial institution under the national law;
  - Apply Basel II standards and principles;
  - Apply ESG criteria;
  - Solid financial standing;
  - Dedicated to green financing or Agri unit or proof of commitment to scale up in green technologies;
  - Sound governance and management;
  - Capacity to manage credit risk;
  - Experience in managing loans;
- **Risk management**
  - Minimum credit rating (national or international);
  - Minimum capital adequacy ratio;
  - Minimum liquidity ratio;
- **Environmental, social and climate safeguards**
  - Adherence to BOAD and GCF environmental, social and climate safeguards;
  - Mitigation of biodiversity loss and climate change impacts related on the agriculture sector;
- **Others**
  - Demonstrable willingness to mainstream gender and youth issues;
  - Compliance with the West African States Central Bank’s regulatory requirements;
  - Any others deemed appropriate by BOAD.

Output 3.4: Control, supervision and monitoring and evaluation of project activities for Euros 1,225,000 of which Euros 1,129,159 from CGI.

This Output will be carried out by the Activity 3.4.1: Control and supervision of works, and the Activity 3.4.2: Monitoring of the Project. These are services which include in particular:
(i) quality control of the works and equipment and the quantities delivered;
(ii) monitoring of the progress of the project and its implementation; (iii) evaluation of the technical and financial aspects of the acceptance of the works. This activity will be carried out by an engineering consulting firm which will be recruited for this purpose. It may be the consultancy firm already called out.

The control and supervision of works is entrusted to the General Directorate of Rural Engineering (DGGER) and its branches with the support of other structures of the Ministry of Agriculture or any other Ministry concerned by the project. To this end, a Control Unit will be created within DGGER. The Control Unit will be headed by an engineer who will be in charge of the Control Unit. The CNEOD in its capacity as DNA will monitor the climatic aspects of the project. Field missions will therefore be carried out to assess the progress and quality of works.

The Environmental Assessment Office (BNEE) will be involved in monitoring the environmental aspects of the project, in particular the impact on biodiversity and the environment. The BNEE will also be responsible for the preparation of the National Environmental Impact Assessment Plan (PNEI) and the Plan for the Management of Pests and Pesticides in collaboration with the Directorate General of Plant Protection.

According to Niger's NDC, the monitoring evaluation and capitalization mechanism will be carried out among others, on the basis of a work plan which will be established by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development. This work plan will evolve in relation to climate change at the national level as well as the capitalisation of experiences and lessons learned from the projects. The M&E system will be based on three levels: (i) the evaluation of the project's results achieved in relation to the set objectives. This will enable the identification of strengths and weaknesses to make many improvements; (ii) the evaluation of the project's impact at national level, which will be carried out by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development for each stage of the process. The project will introduce a self-diagnosis system and report on its progress in relation to the objectives set out in the work plan. The M&E System will also be based on three levels: (i) the evaluation of the project's results achieved at national level; (ii) the evaluation of the project's impact at the regional level, which will be carried out by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development for each stage of the process. The M&E System will also be based on three levels: (iii) the evaluation of the project's results achieved in relation to the set objectives; (iv) the evaluation of the impact of project activities on the target group and the environment, which will be carried out by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development for each stage of the process. The Output will be generated through Activities 3.5.1: Capitalization of results and compilation of lessons learned from the project, Activity 3.5.2: Development of technical and manual sheets for the operator; Activity 3.5.3: Knowledge sharing and dissemination.

The project's monitoring-evaluation system will contribute significantly to managing the performance of the technologies and the traceability of the operations that led to the achievement of the results and to making decisions useful for action. In this way, the lessons learned from the project will be capitalized and archived electronically and physically to strengthen the documentation of lessons learned. The completed technical and manual sheets will be used as a reference guide by the operator and other stakeholders in the sector. This will contribute to strengthening the institutional capacity of the Ministry of Water Resources and Irrigation (MOWR) for intervening in the agricultural sector, particularly the sub-sector of hydrotechnical developments. To allow a better understanding of the project's results, the lessons learned will be disseminated through various channels such as publications, picture guides into and via the official local language of Niger (the results on the private sector financing mechanism will be published in French). The dissemination of these lessons learned will also contribute to the development of criteria for scaling up of the AHA-APC project by the local banks and more financial institutions for the benefit of small, medium and large farmers.

Documents and documentaries on lessons learned and best practices tested under the project on actions to build resilience to climate change in the agricultural sector and irrigation sub-sector will be produced. To this end, the project will develop several fact sheets on the technologies and practices implemented (i.e.) a) fact sheet on the solar water drainage system; b) fact sheet on the maintenance of solar equipment; c) a fact sheet on the sustainable use of groundwater resources in Niger; d) a fact sheet on the profitability of irrigation project activities using modern techniques. These sheets will be designed at the end of the third year and will be disseminated to stakeholders.

The project will support the dissemination of successful results in national policies, plans, programs and projects related to the agricultural sector and irrigation sub-sector. The project will be one of the first to be implemented under the ONHAN Plan (National Action Plan on Climate Change) for Niger. This will contribute significantly to the implementation of the project's strategy. As the use of solar energy on AHA is not yet commonplace, the lessons learnt will make it possible to share knowledge and disseminate good practices for a climate resilient agricultural sector in Niger with Representatives of the Ministry of Water Resources and Irrigation (MOWR), representatives of the National Office for Environmental offices: Private Banks and Microfinance Institutions executives of Niger; Niger's international technical and financial partners, including the World Bank, the European Union, the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) (Nigerians) Initiative Project Management Unit and Executing agency : Ministries in charge of agriculture, plan, and finance. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the National Water Resources Strategy (NWS), Rural Engineering: National Office for Environmental Assessments.

According to the document, describe why this set of interventions was selected instead of alternative solutions and how the project/programme can help unlock the needed support in a sustainable manner.


There are two main alternatives for the project, namely: i) using the current irrigation strategy for the development of AHA; ii) developing small-scale irrigation projects.

Using the current irrigation strategy for the development of AHA without considering climate change related problems. Despite the previous resources mobilized by the Niger government and the various donors, this intervention strategy initiated in 2013 has not been successful because it is based on a technology that is not adapted to the local context. This technology often connected to the electricity network or depended on the fossil fuel energy-based engines which increased the operational costs of irrigation projects. The project will contribute significantly to improving the implementation and sustainability improvement in the implementation and functioning of these AHA by the introduction of clean energy such as solar energy, which is adapted to the local context.

The development of small-scale irrigation projects also highlighted private small-scale irrigation. The development of this irrigation strategy has been supported through the World Bank's PIP project. This irrigation approach was not retained in

Small-scale irrigation

Technical project management approach

The technical project management approach will be based on a phased approach and intervention principles(see Appendix 6) promoting the following:

- Use of a para-state service: DNHA, with the technical capabilities for rapid and real-time intervention to accelerate the implementation of the project/programme.
- Strengthening the institutional capacity of AHA in terms of human resources, financial management, and strengthening the resilience dimensions of AHA to the harmful effects of climate change; (ii) learning and disseminating knowledge on best practices and lessons learned from other projects/programmes implemented by DNHA with a focus on replication of proven technologies and techniques for fighting climate change at the level of each AHA to develop.

Project organization and management

The Executing Entity of the Republic Niger, acting within (i) GNHA and respect to implementation of components 1 and 2, by the Ministry of Agriculture and Water Resources Government of the West Coast with respect to the flow of GCF projects, project coordination, management, and execution.

The various Organizations that will intervene directly in the project are: (i) the Accredited Entity (BOAD); (ii) the National Coordination Committee for Climate Change Adaptation and Sustainable Development (CNCCASD); (iii) the National Contracting Authority for financing, the Ministry of Planning; (iv) the National Project Owner (the Ministry in charge of irrigation); (v) the Technical Service Provider (TSP) - the National Irrigation Office (ONI); (vi) the Implementing Organization: DGNIR; (vii) the implementing entity of project in charge to carry out the work planned (the National Office of Irrigation and Drainage, ONIDR); (viii) the Ministry of Finance and Economic Development; (ix) the Ministry of Agriculture and Water Resources Government of Niger.

Accredited Entity

The BOAD will bring the benefits of WAEEMU Member States, local communities and public and private corporations of the WAEEMU region. The BOAD also collaborates with non-WAEEMU Member State, should their agencies and corporations contribute to the development or integration of the Union's economies. The financing tools used by the BOAD may consist of direct loans, equity investments, guarantees, and other financial instruments such as credit lines, letters of credit, and bond loan guarantees. The BOAD supports the private sector both through the establishment of direct loans to corporates and through the provision of guarantees for corporate debt. In addition, the BOAD provides technical assistance in support of the financing of Small and medium-sized enterprises (SME). This financial support may also be combined with financial services.

These services are deployed within eight different sectors:

- Agriculture and rural development;
