## Infrastructure including energy, water, transportation, land-use and sanitation, telecommunications;

### Finance and insurance;
### Social projects including health and education;
### Economic and commercial services such as real estate operations and leasing, corporate services

In its Strategy for 2021-2025 (DQUBILE), the ROAD intends to become the “Reference Bank” for a lasting impact on the integration and transformation of West Africa. This plan comprises three operational axes:

* Contributing to the creation of value and productive jobs in support of States and the private sector;
* Supporting the development of public infrastructure;
* Strengthening the capacity of States and local authorities

The West African Development Bank (ROAD) is the accredited entity (AE), for the overall implementation of the project. Financial resources from the GCF will be managed according to the general provisions of the AMA between the GCF and ROAD.

* BOARD will be responsible for the overall oversight of the framework, its implementation and will report to GCF as per the terms agreed upon;
* BOARD will report to GCF as per the terms to be agreed under the AMA and the Funded Activity Agreement (FAA). The BOARD will also be responsible for ensuring that the project is in line with its approved safeguard(s)/impact classification, i.e. only up to category B);
* BOARD will be responsible for the identification, formulation, evaluation of the project, allocation of funds, and reports to the GCF on project management;
* BOARD will be responsible for the monitoring and internal controls based on GCF resources in accordance with its procedures and the terms agreed under the Accreditation Management Agreement (AMA).

It is accredited for the implementation of climate change projects under the three financing mechanisms, namely the Pilot Program for Climate Resilience (PPCR), the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and the Special Climate Change Fund (SCCF). The GCF has a dedicated Environment and Climate Finance Unit (ECFU) at the national level. In the case of climate change mitigation and/or adaptation projects, the Bank has a dedicated Environment and Climate Finance Management Unit.

National Designated Authority

The National Commission for Environment and Sustainable Development (CINEDD), is the national designated authority of Niger. The CINEDD will be the implementing authority for the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and CINEDD of Niger will have the role to monitor the alignment of the actions proposed by the project with the national priorities defined in particular in the nationally determined contributions (NDCs). The CINEDD will also ensure that the project is in line with the views of stakeholders around the project (iii) supervise the preparation of the funding proposal; (iv) ensure that the GCF investment proposal is consistent with the Niger’s National Strategic Plan for Climate Change and Sustainable Development (NSPCCSD); (v) provide authorizations at the national level: (vi) provide the letter of non-rejection for the project, (vii) supervise the implementation of the project in accordance with the terms agreed upon between the GCF, the ROAD and the CINEDD. The CINEDD will also ensure that the monitoring and evaluation of the project in relation to the indicators concerning climate change are carried out at least once a year for each project implemented by the Road Fund for the execution of this Project.

National Contracting authority for financing: Ministry of Planning

The Government of Niger is represented by the Ministry of Planning as the financial contracting authority. In this framework, the Ministry of Planning will be responsible for the following: (i) sign the loan agreement with the ROAD as the Accredited Entity; and (ii) the loan Agreement with the BOARD as co-financer of the Project. On behalf of the Republic of Niger, the Ministry of Planning will also be responsible for the implementation of the project in accordance with the terms agreed upon between the GCF, the funding allocated to the project by the GCF, ROAD and the Nigerien State in the government’s investment budget. The Ministry of Planning will also ensure that the project is consistent with the NSPCCSD and the indicators concerning climate change. The Ministry of Planning will be responsible for reporting to the Government on the efficient use of the funds obtained.

The Ministry can also exercise its control by choosing to audit the accounts after the fact. The Ministry of Planning is responsible for reporting to the Government on the efficient use of the funds obtained

# National Project Owner: Ministry of Agriculture

The Government of Niger represented by the Ministry of Agriculture as National Project Owner. The Ministry of Agriculture will be responsible for the implementation of the project in accordance with the continuity of the principle of the ownership of Niger's projects, as well as the implementation of the project in accordance with the provisions of the Multi-Year Contract and the terms and conditions of the Project Implementation Unit (PIU). On behalf of the Government: (i) will set up the Project Steering Committee; (ii) will set up the Project Management Unit at the level of the Director General of Rural Engineering within the Ministry of Agriculture.

## Project Steering Committee (PSC)

The general supervision of the project will be provided by a Steering Committee set up by the Minister of Agriculture's Order. The PSC is composed of: (i) the Minister of Agriculture; (ii) the representative of the Ministry of Finance; (iii) the representative of the Ministry of Water Resources, Environment and Forests; (iv) the representative of the Ministry of Rural Engineering; (v) the General Director of Agriculture; (vi) the representative of the High Commission for the 3 N Invention; (vii) the representative of the Ministry in charge of Finance; (viii) a representative of the Governor of Agadez, Tchad, Maradi, Dosso and Zinder and Diffa (5 representatives in total); (ix) the representative of the Permanent Secretariat of the Rural Code. At the invitation of the President of the NPC, the General Director of ONAH, the Program Managers of the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock as well as the Coordinator of the Project Management Unit, the Heads of the Ministry's various departments will be invited to attend meetings of the PSC.

## Project Execution and Implementation

The National Office for Hydro-Agricultural Development (ONAH) will be responsible for the implementation of the activities of the project in the Niger River Basin which facility will be executed by ONAH. ONAH will sign a specific contract by direct agreement with the Ministry of Agriculture, which represents the Government of Niger. Specifically, ONAH will be responsible for: (i) the preparation and execution of the project; (ii) the supervision of the design and construction of the site development work; (iii) the implementation of stakeholders' capacity-building activities and capitalizing lessons learned; (iv) the monitoring and evaluation of the project's results in terms of the achievement of the project's objectives, on time for the work carried out by ONAH; (v) the preparation, signature and follow-up of the Farming Contract on the basis of the Project Implementation Unit (PIU); (vi) the supervision of the execution of the contract on water management, irrigation infrastructure and equipment with the AUE; (vii) the supervision of the execution of the project's activities in terms of the achievement of the indicators. The Director of Infrastructure and Materials and the Workrooms is the Technical Coordinator of site development work. The Project Management Unit (PMU) will be responsible for: (i) the preparation, signature and follow-up of the Farming Contract; (ii) the supervision of the execution of the contract on water management, irrigation infrastructure and equipment with the AUE; (iii) the supervision of the execution of the project's activities in terms of the achievement of the indicators. The Director of Infrastructure and Materials and the Workrooms is the Technical Coordinator of site development work. The Project Management Unit (PMU) will be responsible for: (i) the preparation, signature and follow-up of the Farming Contract; (ii) the supervision of the execution of the contract on water management, irrigation infrastructure and equipment with the AUE; (iii) the supervision of the execution of the project's activities in terms of the achievement of the indicators. The Director of Infrastructure and Materials and the Workrooms is the Technical Coordinator of site development work.

The Project Management Unit (PMU)

Under the article 1.42.1 of the Planarian Contract (CPP) between ONAH and the State of Niger, the General Director of ONAH will be responsible for the implementation of the project in accordance with the continuity of the principle of the ownership of Niger's projects as well as the implementation of the project in accordance with the terms and conditions of the Project Implementation Unit (PIU). The General Director of ONAH is the Coordinator of the PIU; the Director of Development and Economic Analysis "Direction de la Mise en Vale et des Analyses Economiques" of ONAH is the Technical Coordinator of site development work. The PMU will be responsible for: (i) the preparation, signature and follow-up of the Farming Contract; (ii) the supervision of the execution of the contract on water management, irrigation infrastructure and equipment with the AUE; (iii) the supervision of the execution of the project's activities in terms of the achievement of the indicators. The Director of Infrastructure and Materials and the Workrooms is the Technical Coordinator of site development work.

The PMU members are fully accountable to ONAH which carries the overall responsibility for the implementation of the project. A Project Management Unit (PMU) will be recruited for supporting the monitoring the gender action plan of the project.

The Project Management Unit (PMU) is under the supervision of the General Directorate of Rural Engineering. This PMU has the following responsibilities: (i) preparation and submission of project implementation plans; (ii) monitoring and control of all project activities; (iii) preparation, submission to the NPC, and application of the administrative, financial, and technical documentation for the disbursement of GCF resources; (iv) preparation of the annual work plan and budget (AWPB) of the project, taking into account the needs expressed by the beneficiary communities in the context of the project implementation plans; (v) management of contracts; (vi) monitoring and management of the relationship between the project, the BOAD and the Green Climate Fund until the end of the project; (vii) preparation of the annual financial report for the disbursement of GCF resources; (viii) preparation of requests for funds in accordance with the needs, the texts in force and negotiated with BOAD and the Green Climate Fund until the end of the project; (ix) management of the administrative and financial management of the project; (x) internal management control; (xi) control and support to the DGGRH for the certification of works carried out by GNAHA, (xii) preparation of financial assistance reports; (xiii) monitoring and evaluation of the project.

The PMU staff is expected to include: (i) a National Coordinator (Rural Engineering Engineer - Bac + 5 with 15 years of experience in project management including 10 years of experience in follow-up and evaluation of ANA projects); (ii) an Accountant (Bac + 5 in accounting with 10 years of experience, including 10 years of experience in project management); (iii) a Resources Manager (Bac + 5 in resources management with 10 years of experience); (iv) two Accountants (Bac + 2 in accounting with 10 years of experience each), one with a Category B license and the other with a Category C license; (v) an Engineer in Rural Resources Management with 5 years of experience; (vi) two BEPCs with a Category B license and 10 years of experience, including 5 years of experience in project management; (vii) one BEPC with 7 years of experience; (viii) a security guard (BEPC level with 5 years of experience).

The position of the Monitoring and Evaluation Officer will be made available by the Ministry of Agriculture. The rest of the PMU staff will be recruited on a call for applications.

### LEGAL AGREEMENTS

As the Accredited Entity of the project, BOAD will sign a Trust Agreement with GCF, under which, BOAD will use GCF proceeds to provide:

i) A grant to the Republic of Niger under component 1 and 2, and component 3 (with the exception of sub-activity 3.3.2 which is the Agricultural Loan Facility). A grant agreement will be signed between BOAD and the Niger republic's government.

ii) An Agricultural Loan Facility for the implementation of activity 3.3.2 under Output 3.3: Setting-up of an attractive climate resilient agricultural sector in Niger, through a loan facility to the local financial institutions which will lend to beneficiaries for climate-smart agriculture activities. BOAD will be the counterparty of this loan agreement with the selected local financial institutions.

iii) The grant agreement between BOAD and the Niger's Government through the Ministry of the Plan, will include legal arrangements with all the institutions receiving GCF funding to manage the project in strict compliance with BOAD procedures and GCF rules.

### BOAD Co-financing

BOAD will approve a loan for the co-financing of the project. As Co-financee, BOAD will approve a loan for the co-financing of the project. The loan will be approved prior to the disbursement of GCF's resources. BOAD will sign a loan agreement with the Republic of Niger for its own co-financing.

### Subsidiary agreements

As Accredited Entity and Co-financee, the BOAD will sign the following subsidiary agreement with the Government of Niger (the "GCF Grant Agreement") with respect to the management of GCF grant funding in accordance with the FAA.

### B.5.2. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately two pages)

#### i) Niger is ranked the poorest country in the world according to the UN Human Development Index (HDI). As a Least Developed Country located at the heart of the Sahel and one of the poorest countries in the world with limited fiscal capacity, Niger has been unable to finance its climate change adaptation needs. The Republic of Niger spends total financing of EUR 30.187 M from GCF; EUR 24.246.772 as grant and EUR 5.940.000 in the form of a concessional loan through local financial institutions for climate smart agriculture finance. Access to this grant and the credit line are critically needed and will play a significant role in increasing farm productivity for agricultural groups.

#### ii) The most affected sector by climate change is agriculture, which is the main source of income and livelihoods for more than 87% of the population in Niger. As a result, food insecurity, poverty and malnutrition are increasing. Nearly 29.1% of the population lives below the international poverty line (USD 1.90 per day) and nearly 54% live on less than USD 3.20 per day.

## ii) The COVID-19 pandemic necessarized concessional prospects and the impact of climate change on food security.

According to the African Development Bank, in the context of COVID-19, real GDP growth will decline from 7% in 2018 to -0.7% in 2020. The budget allocated to agriculture by the government remains well below its pre-COVID level. In addition, the current account balance deficit is projected at 5.3% of GDP in 2020 and the current account balance from 64.9% in 2018 to -15.9% in 2020*. The budget deficit, estimated at 7.1% of GDP in 2019, is projected to reach 13.1% of GDP in 2020 and 14.6% in 2021. The current account balance deficit set at 5% in the West African Economic and Monetary Union (UEMOA). As early as June 2018, the sustainability of public finances was already under threat due to a combination of high fiscal pressures and tightening financial conditions.

The effects of economic conditions on Niger's fiscal balance have been compounded by COVID-19 and make additional sovereign debt even more difficult to assume.* The budget allocated to agriculture by the government remains well below its pre-COVID level. In addition, the current account balance deficit is projected at 5.3% of GDP in 2020 and the current account balance from 64.9% in 2018 to -15.9% in 2020*. The budget deficit, estimated at 7.1% of GDP in 2019, is projected to reach 13.1% of GDP in 2020 and 14.6% in 2021.

### Justification for Agricultural Loan Facility to mobilize private sector financing for climate smart agriculture through the local financial sector

While agriculture contributes more than 30% of GDP and employs almost 85% of Niger's working population, the proportion of agricultural credit allocated to agriculture is extremely limited (less than 1% of total lending). The factors that hinder the development of appropriate and accessible on-farm financial services are: high credit interest rates with short-term maturities and high transaction costs. These factors make it difficult for farmers to access credit in a timely manner, especially in rural sectors/activities due to the high risk perception and difficulties in debt collection (lack of guarantee mechanisms), and the lack of capacity of Banks and Microfinance Institutions (MFIs) on financing agriculture groups and cooperatives for climate smart agriculture.

Interest rates in the banking and microfinance institutions sector that can reach 20% and this for a short-term loan. This explains the incredibly low penetration of agricultural credit. Thus, it is necessary for the sustainable financing of Nigerien agriculture to

**G**

Thus, the choice of a grant and concessional loan is made because the beneficiaries of the project are mainly poor groups vulnerable to climate change and food insecurity. The majority of these groups have no access to financial services or credit. Therefore, GCF financing provided through local financial institutions will be able to provide access to affordable and long-term loans for agriculture groups and cooperatives.

### Justification of using grants and loans for the same output and details in terms of what types of expenses to be financed by GCF grants and BOAD loans

GCF is financing the project with grants (except Output 3.3) and loan (except Output 3.3) (the lending facility to local financial institutions). BOAD will finance the non-revenue generating component each of the project except Output 3.3. It is one project with different activities, the non-revenue generating activities are not totally separated from the activities with revenue-generating potential. The project seeks to strengthen sustainability of vulnerable groups in the face of adverse effects of climate change and food insecurity by: (i) rehabilitating 500 ha each year through a pluriannual plan contract signed with the National Office of Irrigation Agricultural Cooperatives (ONACI); (ii) providing training for local farmers on water management techniques; (iii) establishing an initiative "Nigerian Feed Ngonons" (NNF); (iv) the development and implementation of a national policy to enhance the resilience of local communities to adverse effects of climate change. The project will also provide technical assistance to the Government of Niger for the same outputs in order to support the overall bankruptcy of the project while reducing the debt burden on the government. The project is expected to generate revenue through the sale of agricultural products and services funded by GCF grants and BOAD loans, respectively. (Annex 4: Budget Excel has been provided to the Finance team).

### EEX strategy and sustainability (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The project seeks to strengthen sustainability of vulnerable groups in the face of adverse effects of climate change and food insecurity by: (i) rehabilitating 500 ha each year through a pluriannual plan contract signed with the National Office of Irrigation Agricultural Cooperatives (ONACI); (ii) providing training for local farmers on water management techniques; (iii) establishing an initiative "Nigerian Feed Ngonons" (NNF); (iv) the development and implementation of a national policy to enhance the resilience of local communities to adverse effects of climate change. The project will also provide technical assistance to the Government of Niger for the same outputs in order to support the overall bankruptcy of the project while reducing the debt burden on the government.

All the economic and financial level, the implementation of the promoted techniques and technologies will allow producers to improve agricultural yields by at least 30% and to increase from 1 to 2 agricultural campaigns or from 2 to 3 agricultural campaigns per year. The promotion of energy efficient irrigation systems and the use of renewable sources of energy will enable farmers' groups and cooperatives to make the profits to cover the costs of maintaining and replacing the equipment, as well as the cost of water supply. The project will also promote the establishment of credit unions and agricultural input mechanisms for the maintenance of operating infrastructure. With flood protection actions, the project aims at improving access to irrigation water by increasing the availability of water in the dry season and reducing the risk of drying out plots and increasing production and income. Better management of water resources will increase the productivity of crops and livestock while reducing costs.

Reducing plot operations costing and improving yields are likely to improve producers' incomes. The project targets more women's groups who are generally good managers. This will ensure the sustainability of the actions.

### Vulnerable farmers' groups and cooperatives have many difficulties to access low cost financing for climate resilient agriculture

- (i) high interest rates charged by banks and MFIs on lending products for climate smart agriculture
- (ii) limited availability of credit lines for climate smart agriculture
- (iii) high interest rates charged by banks and MFIs on lending products for climate smart agriculture
- (iv) low capacity of financial institutions to provide credit lines for climate smart agriculture
- (v) high interest rates in the banking and microfinance institutions sector can reach to 20% and for a short-term loan. This explains the limited availability of credit lines for climate smart agriculture
- (vi) lack of awareness among farmers on the benefits of climate smart agriculture

- (viii) high interest rates in the banking and microfinance institutions sector can reach to 20% and for a short-term loan. This explains the limited availability of credit lines for climate smart agriculture

- (x) low capacity of financial institutions to provide credit lines for climate smart agriculture
- (xi) high interest rates in the banking and microfinance institutions sector can reach to 20% and for a short-term loan. This explains the limited availability of credit lines for climate smart agriculture

### The project will support the development of a replicable model of climate-smart agriculture in Niger through the following activities:

1. Building the capacity of financial institutions to provide credit lines for climate smart agriculture.
2. Setting up an attractive on-lending mechanism for climate smart agriculture through local financial institutions in the form of an Agricultural Loan Facility (ALF) with a revolving fund of 6 million euros.
3. Providing technical assistance and training to farmers, agricultural cooperatives, and other projects, with the support of regional or international development financial institutions (the "Agricultural Loan Facility").

### The financial sector in Niger has over USD 21 billion in total assets, hence constitutes an important source of finance to support climate smart agriculture. The project will support the development of a replicable model of climate-smart agriculture with solar energy, innovative irrigation techniques.

## Baseline Emissions:

The baseline emissions for powering irrigation systems in agricultural value chains in Niger are using diesel generators.

Solar systems are capital intensive at start up and farmers do not see the need to invest in expensive technology.
Without this project, irrigation systems in Ahinké would be powered by diesel generators. The average size of the generator is 10kW.

The baseline emissions as per CDI ASM/IF are therefore the annual electricity generated by the renewable energy units minus that which would have been generated by the diesel generators. If the diesel generators were replaced with Solar for Diesel generators would have run at 50% of their capacity as explained above.
As the baseline diesel generator capacity in all cases will be below 15kW and the generators will be operated for an average of 9 hours daily, the emissions from these systems are negligible. The total annual generation is therefore less than 20 MWh (Please see annex 22B). These baseline emissions are therefore summarized in the following table:

| Year | Electricity Generated by the Renewable | 4,558 MW*1740 hours = 7,963.12 MWh |
| --- | --- | --- |

### Energy Systems:
- **Efficiency Factor of Diesel on Electricity:** 1.0 CDI/20MWh
- **Resulting yearly baseline emissions for electricity:** 11,176.3 ICDI eq

## Project's Own Emissions:

The project will install solar PV systems and will not have any diesel generators running concurrently or as a backup.
Therefore the project will not contribute to the production of any emissions through power generation.

Figure 30 below shows the approach taken, which is the complete displacement of fossil fuel. There will also be no electricity consumption from the grid; therefore no leakage.

### Leakage Emissions:

No leakage emissions are associated with the funded activity, as only new equipment will be installed.

## Emissions Reduction calculation assumptions:

Given that the project will not generate emissions nor have any leakage.
Emissions Reduction are Baseline Emissions in the absence of GCF project.

The project's aggregated installed solar capacity is 4,558 MWp which will generate electricity for an average yearly utilization rate of 1740 hours including system efficiencies as provided in the map in fig2a and fig 2b of annex 22B.
As can be seen on fig.2a, the yearly total irradiation in the project area is between 1,750 kWh/m² and 1,826 kWh/m² which translates to a total annual generation of approximately 4,390 MWh.
The solar PV systems will operate for an average daily cycle length of 4.4 hours,
which translates to a total annual generation of 1,740 hours.

# Monitoring and reporting arrangements

The monitoring and reporting frequency will be annual. Sampling will be done to cover at least 10% of the systems installed in each country. The sampling will be done through a representative sample of GEF-managed projects. The data will be monitored to monitor the energy generated by the solar power systems. This will be done through energy meters on at least 10% of the solar power systems to monitor the amount of energy generated.

Other monitoring and reporting arrangements include: (i) regular reporting of project progress against the schedule of site development actions and GHG reduction data collection; (ii) GEF National Designated Authority (NDA), CNEED will supervise the collection of the data by GNAKH. The results will be sent to the Project management unit (PMU), which will present them in the annual report submitted to the Accredited Entity (AE).

## D.2 Paradigm shift potential (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Describe the degree to which the proposed activity can catalyze impact beyond a one-off project or programme implementation.

* Potential for scaling up and replication

The AfRisks are facing: (i) critical recurring and increasingly pronounced adverse effects of climate change, namely irregular rainfall, shorter rainy season, droughts, floods, lack of water availability for cropping during the rainy season; (ii) increasing GHG emissions from agriculture due to inefficient irrigation techniques; and (iii) lack of sustainable private sector funding for cost-effective production.

The proposed climate smart agriculture project will be one of the first of its kind on irrigated Agriculture Developments (ADs) in Africa. The project is expected to have a significant impact beyond the project area, as it will be scaled up across Niger, West African region and beyond. The unique model AfRisks will be climate resilient with GEF support. It will also contribute to: (i) increasing food security for smallholder farmers; (ii) addressing the problem of food insecurity and improve farmers' groups and cooperatives revenues. The project is well designed to address the three major risks facing AfRisks, namely: (i) increasing GHG emissions from agriculture due to increasing of GHG emission on AfRisks; (ii) improve food security and the livelihoods of farmers; and (iii) build private sector investment in climate smart agriculture through the mobilization of the financial sector and iv) increase the participation of women and youth in food security.

The irrigation areas of the AfRisks will be developed based on scalable and replicable climate resilient and GHG reduction technologies. The project is expected to have a significant impact beyond the project area, as it will be climate smart agriculture business model in this project will be cost-effective through: (i) optimal organization for land management; (ii) efficient water use; (iii) improved soil fertility; (iv) increased crop yields and productivity; (v) crop rotation itineraries adapted to the major climatic risks of irrigated areas for crop production. In addition, the project will provide income opportunities for women and youth through the development of small-scale enterprises in the AfRisks.

GCF funds will local financial institutions to extend loans at lower interest rates and longer tenors to end beneficiaries. The farmers' groups and cooperatives technical capacities, knowledge, and farming practices resilient to climate change will be improved by providing training on climate-smart agriculture techniques. Women and youth will acquire more productive capacity and resilience to the adverse effects of climate change.

Complementarily, GCF loans will contribute to the development of climate-smart credit scoring tools that ensure that loan applications are appropriately screened to reduce risks to the lenders, thereby also contributing to the reduction


---
**Page 59**
---

In interest rate terms, it is expected that GCF’s interventions will reduce significantly reduce interest rates from 20% to at most 15% in the world case in the long term. Subsequently, financial sector loans will gradually become more attractive on a competitive basis and will be able to provide greater access to credit for smallholder farmers and other vulnerable groups in resilient agriculture and create a self-sustaining loan portfolio that ensures the adoption at scale of climate-smart agricultural practices in the long run.

The financial sector in Niger has over USD 2.1 billion in total assets, hence, constitutes an important source of finance for the project. The GCF will leverage this investment capital for climate-smart agriculture. The credit line financed by the GCF will serve as a lever to mobilize significant low-cost financial resources for Niger’s banks and financial institutions. The introduction of innovative financing mechanisms such as blended finance, green bonds, and microfinance instruments will enable the development of a replicable model of climate-smart agriculture financing combined with solar energy investment in rural areas. This will ensure that the project is financially sustainable over time, which will enhance its concessionality will be passed on to end beneficiaries.

Decisions makers in the financial institutions will acquire a better understanding of microfinance profiles of farming clients and the associated risks, as well as the ability to implement appropriate governance, lending procedures and credit risk tools that reduce their lending risks. In the long term, capital returns on loan repayments from smallholder farmers will be reinvested into the project’s revolving fund for further investments in renewable energy sources to achieve greater investment in agricultural resilience in Niger. The socio-economic and environmental benefits of this project are expected to be significant as it will increase access to clean energy, reduce poverty and diffusion of best practices and the interventions of the project to facilitate the upscaling and replication of these climate-smart agricultural practices in other parts of Niger. The financial institutions will also benefit from the technical capacity which will also further enable local financial institutions to raise additional funds from development finance organizations (DFOs) for the scaling-up of this project.

In fact, to combat the adverse effects of climate change, Nigeria’s NDC prioritizes adaptation, particularly in the Agriculture sector. The NDC states that 80% of its total GHG emissions are due to land use and land-use change, that the AFOLU and energy sectors account for 89% and 61% of Niger’s total GHG emissions respectively. So whether in terms of climate change mitigation or adaptation, the NDC is clear on the need for Niger to increase access to modern energy services for all by 2030. To achieve this vision of the NDC requires all national and regional stakeholders to work together to ensure that the country can meet its commitments under the Paris Agreement to achieve food security and increase farmers income, in their agricultural development policies, programs, and projects. This project will contribute significantly towards meeting these goals by providing a replicable model which requires all agricultural development national and international actors to include these climate-smart farming technologies as part of their investment projects following the model of the current project. This will ensure the scaling up of the current project.

The need for sustainable management of Aïna in a context of climate change is particularly important in Niger. Unfortunately, this need is not covered due to a lack of means, among others, technical and financial. Despite all the environmental and economic benefits that would result from the implementation of the AHA-AIC project (i.e., AHA-AIC Project) will be able to cover only a relatively small part of the overall needs. While the AHA-AIC Project is not expected to have any significant impact on food security in Niger, it is important to note that the challenge for the coming years will be to adapt this approach at the national level if food insecurity is to be tackled by 2035.

This is obviously an opportunity for the sustainable financing of Nigerian agriculture through its private financial sector to play a role. The AHA-AIC Project will provide experience in this area. Current financial institutions can therefore meet these needs if the issue of financing their own agricultural activities at incentive rates is resolved.

The following actions are particularly favorable to the promotion of a private and sustainable financing mechanism for agriculture resilient to climate change will be created through Output 3.1, of Component 3 of the project for scaling up AHA-AIC Project. The creation of this mechanism will be based on the development of an innovative financial mechanism that will set out from the current public financing provided by the State of Niger, the BOAD and the GCF to finance agricultural activities resilient to climate change. This mechanism will be designed in such a way as to create an enabling environment to prepare stakeholders for the promotion of climate-resilient agricultural finance mechanisms. The following actions are proposed: (a) Activity 3.1.1. Set up a financial mechanism to provide subsidies interest rates and stimulate innovation in terms of dedicated financial products and services. These activities will be carried out by the BOAD, the GCF and the State of Niger; (b) Activity 3.2.1. Set up an enabling environment for climate smart agriculture: (c) Activity 3.2.2. Set up a public-private partnership mechanism to finance agricultural activities resilient to climate smart agriculture; (d) Activity 3.2.3. Set up an attractive on-lending mechanism for climate-smart agriculture products and services, which will be scaled up at a later stage, in the context of other projects, with the support regional or international development funds. The creation of this financial mechanism will be based on the experience gained from the AHA-AIC Project and the promotion of a private and sustainable financing mechanism for agriculture resilient to climate change is created for the scaling up of the AHA-AIC project, included in component 3.

### Contribution to the regulatory framework

To create a favorable legal operating environment, the project will establish a water and energy management framework. The project planned at activity 3.1.4. The improvement of laws binding the State, Obligations and cooperatives and ensuring the implementation of the AHA-AIC Project. This will be done by integrating the following actions: (a) the integration in the operating contract of the provisions relating to the technical, financial and organizational aspects of the project; (b) the integration in the operating contract of the provisions relating to the rehabilitation of the introduction of new AHAs; (c) Techniques and technologies related to the adaptation of AHAs to climate change will be integrated into the operating contract of the AHA-AIC Project. These actions are aimed at: (a) the reduction of greenhouse gases; (b) the integration in the operating contract between ONHAA and the AEU, the inclusion of provisions relating to the rehabilitation of the introduction of new AHAs; (c) the strengthening of the resilience of the AHA; (d) maintenance and sustainable management of solar equipment and other GHG reduction technologies. The project will also establish a framework for the implementation of a collaboration contract between ONHAA and the AEU, taking into account: (a) maintenance and sustainable management of solar equipment and other GHG reduction technologies; (b) strengthening the resilience of the AHA; (c) maintenance and sustainable management of solar equipment and other GHG reduction technologies. The project will also establish a framework for the implementation of a collaboration contract between ONHAA and the AEU, taking into account: (a) maintenance and sustainable management of solar equipment and other GHG reduction technologies; (b) strengthening the resilience of the AHA; (c) maintenance and sustainable management of solar equipment and other GHG reduction technologies.

AHAs are facing land problems. This project establishes a methodology to promote AHAs land registration to secure their rights over the land they occupy, in accordance with Article 29 of the Organic Law on Land (Law No. 01/2008 of January 30, 2008) and Article 295.

In view of the expected results in the implementation of the project including water savings and GHG emissions reductions, the lessons learned from the project will encourage the Nigerian government to promote a regulatory framework for the promotion of climate-resilient agricultural activities. This will be done by promoting change in terms of clean energy and water savings, incentives such as subsidizing the cost of agricultural equipment, improving crop yields, limiting soil erosion, reducing post-harvest loss of crops, etc.

The results of the combination of technologies show that the overall productivity remains high (0.84) by adaptation (0.86) and finally mitigation (-0.43). This shows that the combination contributes effectively to the achievement of the triple ambition of climate-smart agriculture.

Compared to technologies taken in isolation, the highest increase is observed for the combination of technology and adaptation (1.29), followed by the combination of technology and mitigation (0.86). The combination of all three techniques (technology, adaptation, and mitigation) contributes effectively and sustainably to the achievement of climate-smart agriculture.

# D.3 Sustainable development (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Describe the wider benefits and priorities of the project/programme in relation to the Sustainable Development Goals and the Paris Agreement on Climate Change.

- Environmental co-benefits

The environmental co-benefits of this project are: sustainable management of water resources; reducing fossil fuel consumption and greenhouse gas emissions; improving or maintaining soil quality and maintaining biodiversity. 

Sustainable management of water resources and their quality: The irrigation system currently practiced on AHA consists mainly of gravity-fed canals, which have a high loss rate (30-45%). This project will replace the existing canal network with an irrigation system based on a pump station that will supply water to the perimeter. The new irrigation system will reduce the losses by 70% compared to the old non-efficient practices.

If the old non-efficient practices are renewed, the water losses will amount to 30 million per year for the 15,500 ha of land irrigated in the project area. If the new efficient practices are implemented, the promoted system has a return of 85% compared to 60% in the old system, a gain of 25%, i.e., 18 million per year.

Soil and Crop Protection: Strengthening the support of technical services and training producers is good practice for soil protection. The project devotes part of its Component B to the protection of productive capital against threats such as pests and diseases. This will be done by providing producers with a wide range of inputs in areas to be developed as well as planting trees. Planting actions with local species and the practice of agroforestry will help to improve soil quality, reduce erosion and increase biodiversity.

The project will promote soil quality, facilitate carbon storage in the soil and maintain local biodiversity.

The social benefits of the project include: (i) Better access for energy to farmers; (ii) Improving the food security of producers by increasing their productivity; (iii) Improving health and safety; (iv) Improved access to education; (v) Reducing the phenomenon of rural exodus.


---
**Page 63**
---

# Better access to energy for irrigation

The promotion of solar energy as part of the project will increase producers' access to safe energy services. After water, energy is the second largest factor in agricultural production in Niger. Improved access to energy services will allow producers to use more efficient and productive equipment, enabling them to produce crops. Producers using generators must be able to permanently diesel. By promoting the use of solar energy as part of the project, producers will have allowed investments on the selected irrigation areas to have continuous, safer and better energy access.

Improving the food security and access to education: The implementation of the FAO and the development of 1000 hectares of irrigated land in addition to the promotion of sustainable agricultural practices and the creation of jobs for both food and feed producers and women will contribute significantly to improving the food security of Niger. In addition, the project will allow the use of renewable energy and the intelligent use of water resources to avoid disaster for future generations.

Improving health and access to education: Maternal and food insecurity affect the health and safety of people in rural areas. The project will provide a significant contribution to improving maternal and child health by providing insurance of finding their sufficient source of water or fossil fuel resources meet their agricultural development. The project will enable the use of renewable energy and the intelligent use of water resources to avoid disaster for future generations.

Reducing the phenomenon of rural exodus: The project will be implemented by the beneficiary populations. It will provide them with a significant contribution in terms of employment, income generation, migration into more developed countries; the project will therefore help to limit the phenomenon of rural exodus.

Economic benefits are linked to job creation (reducing energy bills); (improving revenue accounts); (reducing imports and improving the trade balance); (by reducing the budget deficit).

The project will also contribute significantly to increasing the number of jobs created by providing major local labor and indirect jobs along the sites and supply and distribution channels for raw and semi-finished agricultural products. Approximately 12,000 people are expected to be employed directly or indirectly in the course of the implementation of this project; the project will enable the development of economic marketing channels as well as product processing and conservation activities.

Reduction of energy bills: Since the price of diesel at the pump is $0.82/Euros per liter in Niger, the annual savings on the energy bill for the 1500 h of AHA will be 1,837,734 Euros, or 73 €/Euro 940 euros over 20 years, or another 20 years. The project will also contribute to reducing the cost of production by allowing producers to reduce household saving on the energy bill for agricultural production will be 154.31 Euros per year (per beneficiary), i.e., 3086.2 Euros over a period of 20 years.

Improved operating accounts and capacity to take out a loan bank for agriculture resilient activities: The project will contribute significantly to improving the operating accounts of producers by reducing the costs of production factors (water, electricity, inputs, etc.), improve agricultural yields and increase the number of hectares cultivated per campaign. It will also reduce the price of production.

On other AHA's it contributes to reducing the adverse effects of climate change on an expensive thermal electric energy source that emits greenhouse gases; farmers' benefits are marginal and sometimes are null or negative in the event of a drought.

The development of AHA with innovative climate-smart agriculture techniques ensure both the resilience of cultivated areas against the perverse effects of climate change, the mitigation of greenhouse gas, food and nutrition security, income generation and poverty reduction.

- A significant improvement in production and yields between 21% and 78% according to specifications;

### D.4 Needs of recipient (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

Describe the scale and intensity of vulnerability of the country and beneficiary groups (e.g., the level of exposure to climate change, droughts, floods, etc.), overall income levels, etc.; how this project/proposal addresses the issue (Describe specific actions taken in the following needs).

#### Economic and social development level of the country and affected populations

This project is being implemented on irrigated land in several countries that are at different stages of human development index and in one of the most arid and drought-prone areas in the world. The area covered by the Sahara Desert, Niger in the north (Nigerien Sahel Development Project - NSDP), Chad (Project for the Rehabilitation of the Human Development Index) and the Lower Congo River Basin (LRCRB) is among the countries targeted under this project.

The country's economy is mainly based on agriculture. The main source of income and livelihoods, the negative effects of climate change are felt most severely in rural areas where the majority of the population lives. The country has specific vulnerabilities, including gender gaps for adaptation only.

From 1983 to 2014, the average temperature rose by about 1.6°C (2.9°F) with a significant increase in rainfall occurring with isohyet sliding southwards. There is a sequence of deficit years with implications for agricultural production. At a national level, there has been a decline in cereal and livestock production since the 1980s; domestic agricultural production is currently structurally in deficit due to climate change.

According to climate projections, Niger will face: (i) an average temperature rise on average of 1.6-2.9°C by 2050 with the

## Increased intensity of rainfall during June and September (during wintering) [20], an increase in the frequency and intensity of rainfall during the second half of the rainy season (July–October) by 2050; [20]), an increase in the interannual and intraseasonal variability of precipitation, leading to more frequent droughts between dry and wet years; and, h) increased frequency and intensity of torrential rains as well as drought pockets.

These risks could undermine the Nigerian government's efforts and investments to reduce food insecurity and combat climate change. The GCF project will support the implementation of adaptation measures and programmes that show that the country’s priority is to strengthen the resilience of the agriculture and water sectors.

This project is part of Nigeria’s country programme, to mobilize funding from the GCF to promote intelligent farming technologies and practices in order to reduce food insecurity and combat climate change. The project will help to reduce water stress from rising temperatures and increased drought, while ensuring the sustainability of national water resources. It will also support the development of a national adaptation plan for agriculture and water sectors.

The integration of climate information into the development of hydroelectric developments should reduce impacts [20]) on the agricultural sector.

At the local level, the beneficiaries of the project are vulnerable farmers groups and cooperatives, especially small producers of wheat, maize, rice and vegetables. Soil low, poverty levels of irrigation and low agricultural areas, which are prone to droughts and floods, are also targeted by this project.

Soils are low in nutrients and organic matter, with low water retention capacity and vulnerable to erosion; and therefore transported to cause silting of hydro-agricultural developments. These phenomena will be exacerbated by the increase in temperature and rainfall intensity. The project will support the implementation of sustainable soil management increasing soil degradation and increasingly limited (fallow) restoration opportunities. The project will improve soil fertility, reduce soil erosion and water runoff, and increase crop yields.

The project will also support the development of a national adaptation plan for agriculture and water sectors to farmers' groups and cooperatives to facilitate the implementation of sustainable soil restoration options are also planned.

Producers’ adaptation strategies are also extremely limited by access to irrigation (approximately 0.1% of irrigated land), adequate seed and fertilizers. Rising temperatures, increased precipitation irregularity and increased temperature variability will reduce crop yields and increase food insecurity in Nigeria. The project will support the development of a national adaptation plan for agriculture and water sectors.

The project will also support the development of a national adaptation plan for agriculture and water sectors to farmers' groups and cooperatives. Prospered pockets of drought, early end-of-wet season, and damage from crop pests during the 2015 crop year have created production deficits, causing food insecurity and increased poverty levels in Nigeria.

The agricultural calendar with increasing variability in precipitation will certainly be impacted in the medium term. The reduction in rainfall intensity and frequency will reduce water availability for irrigation and increase soil erosion.

### Absence of alternative sources of financing (e.g. fiscal balance or payment gap that prevents government from investing in agriculture)

Niger, a poor country, does not have a local capital mobilization market. Access to financing is through loans with relatively high interest rates. National banks are reluctant to invest in agriculture because of the risks associated with climate change and reducing external funding and putting them available for financial agricultural adaptation is therefore excessively difficult.

## U.S. Country Ownership (max. two words; approximately 1 Page)

The project will contribute to the implementation of the 2017-2020 Four-Year Contract/Plan between the Niger Delta States and the Federal Government of Nigeria, which is aimed at sustainable rural development by taking the environmental dimension into account in my decision that affects the design, planning and implementation of projects. The project will also contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Strategy (SDSC) while implementing the Detailed Development Plan for Nnewi State. It will also contribute to the achievement of the National Environmental Policy (NEP). It stipulates that appropriate solutions must be found to reduce the frequency of annual and/or forest fires, improve soil fertility, increase agricultural productivity, and ensure their accessibility to all social categories in the country in a context of increasing population pressure, the need for land use diversification and intensification, and the need to integrate environmental protection with economic development. This project is in line with SDSC ambitions and objectives and will contribute primarily to strengthening rural households.

The Sustainable Development and Inclusive Growth Strategy (SDICG) Vision 2035 promoted by the Ministry of Planning, Land Development and Community Development, engages forward thinking: to build a knowledge base on climate change adaptation and mitigation; to develop an integrated approach for sustainable development aimed at sustainably increasing agricultural production and productivity to strengthen agricultural Rural households.


---
**Page 67**
---

This project is one of the projects identified by Niger’s country programme to mobilize funding from the GCF to implement adaptation and mitigation projects.

The project is consistent with Niger's National Determined Contribution (NDC), particularly on promoting climate-smart agricultural development in vulnerable areas, and it contributes directly to the achievement of the national targets at the grassroots level. It meets CSA’s three objectives: building resilience (adaptation), reducing GHG emissions from the sector, and increasing productivity (mitigation). The project will contribute to the achievement of the national targets on agricultural developments with climate-resilient techniques and a solar water pumping system to replace thermal power generation in the AfOLU sector. It will also contribute to the achievement of the national targets on reducing GHG emissions from the AFOLU sector, as defined by the NDC, through the implementation of climate-smart agriculture practices (CSA) that will contribute to the achievement of the results associated with the national priorities of the AFOLU sector, of water conservation and management, and of the reduction in GHG emissions. The project will also contribute to the achievement of the national targets on agricultural development with climate-resilient techniques and a solar water pumping system to replace thermal power generation in the AfOLU sector being, in fact, constituted by the results of implementation and scaling up of CSA activities (climate-smart agriculture practices) that will contribute to the achievement of the national priorities of the AFOLU sector, such as: conservation; recovery of degraded land; improvement of the overall forage balance and of food and nutritional security.)

The designated national authority Green Climate Fund (NEEDS of Niger) will have the role of: (i) monitoring the project implementation in close collaboration with the implementing agency; (ii) ensuring that the project is aligned to the National Adaptation Plan Change (RNAA); (iii) mobilize stakeholders around the project; (iv) oversee the preparation of the Action Plan for Climate Resilient Agricultural Development (APACR); (v) ensure the implementation of the APACR and facilitate the administrative procedures for obtaining the necessary authorizations at the national level; (vi) provide the technical support to the implementing agency in the preparation of the project document, including the involvement of organizations (men and women) actively contributed to the preparation of the project through the public consultation process. The project will be implemented by a consortium led by the Ministry of Agriculture, with consultations, vulnerable groups expressed their interest and support for the project activities. To this end, they are the main stakeholders in the project.

D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Describe how the financial structure is adequate and reasonable to achieve the project’s objectives, including identifying any potential risks that could affect the project’s ability to ensure that it will be viable without crowding out private and other public investments. Refer to section B.7 on the justification of GCF funding requirements.

The Government of Niger wishes to carry out climate-resilient hydro-agricultural developments (as stipulated in the National Adaptation Plan Change) in vulnerable areas, which are characterized by low rainfall and poor soil fertility. Through a GCF grant has been requested, to compensate for the lack of financial capacity of the State and to avoid crowding out private investments. The project will contribute to the achievement of the national targets on agricultural development with climate-resilient techniques and a solar water pumping system to replace thermal power generation in vulnerable populations to the climate. The project activities will improve agricultural production to reduce the vulnerability of smallholder farmers, particularly women and youth, to climate change impacts through the use of anti-erosion structures. Taken together, these activities will support and promote sustainable agricultural development in vulnerable areas.

The effectiveness of interventions will be ensured using climate-smart agriculture practices, informed by studies by the Advisory Group for International Agricultural Research (CGAIR), the AGRIHYMET Regional Centre, etc., involving a wide range of stakeholders including farmers, women’s groups, youth organizations, etc. The lifespan of 30 years and its maintenance is relatively inexpensive. The profitability of the developed irrigation system will be ensured through the sale of agricultural products by smallholder producers; in the short term, it shall be able to bear the costs of replacing irrigation systems with a lifespan of 15 to 20 years.

The effectiveness of the project will be further enhanced using best practices and lessons learned from ongoing and previous projects. The project will also benefit from the results of the Niger Climate Change Adaptation Investment Study (CCAS) in this project. In addition, a report on best practices and lessons learned from this project (Output 1.2), as well as an action plan for climate-resilient agricultural development (APACR) will be developed to ensure that there is knowledge sharing between technical services and local communities. Such sharing will further increase the efficiency of the project.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 78 OF 95

## Activity: Drip Irrigation Systems

### 1.1.2 Rehabilitation of 749 hectares of the soil units.

Rehabilitation will involve the sites of Djénojou, I and Gourou, which are particularly vulnerable to the effects of climate change, which seriously compromise their productivity. The average rainfall recorded each year will therefore be a question of adaptation and resilience to the adverse effects of climate change.

### 1.2.1 Activity: Drip Irrigation Systems equipped with automatic installation of drip irrigation systems

Niger being a Sahelian country the flow of surface water in rivers is low, except at the start of the Niger River. These waters are subject to high evaporation and infiltration losses. The difference between farmers and herders limiting the supply of water for irrigation. This project proposes to use relatively abundant groundwater resources by drilling depths during drilling and to develop total control of these resources.

The irrigation system on most Alléka is classic type with a low level of efficiency (recorded levels of water below 50% in the irrigated areas). The temperatures currently experienced in Niger are increasing, which will lead to an increase in evaporation. This will have repercussions for the sources of the surface waters. These masses may become insufficient and there could be a rise in temperature in the coming decades.

To limit these losses and achieve water savings, the project proposes to install drip irrigation systems.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V2 | PAGE 77 OF 95

## Activity: Establishing boreholes with renewable energy systems.

### 1.2.2
This activity consists of equipping boreholes with solar panels and batteries to provide power. This solar energy will replace the diesel generators currently used for pumping water.

### 2.1.1
Securing land is a key element in the sustainable development of developed areas. It secures access to resources, including land for agriculture and livestock farming. In accordance with the laws and acts in force in Niger, the project will be implemented on the basis of the new AHA (which will be operated under its supervision).

### 2.1.2
The project will support the processing for the registration of the application submitted by the beneficiary to the land administration authorities. The project will also provide advice and technical assistance to beneficiaries.

#### Activity: Conducting land registration activities at selected sites

After the development work, the project will divide the project area into plots with a surface area of 15 ha each in the commune of Kouré and 20 ha each in the communes of Gouin and Tchintabaraden. The project will support (i) up to one plot per beneficiary, (ii) an access rate of at least 80% for beneficiaries on plots with a surface area greater than or equal to 15 ha, and (iii) all plots will be allocated as a producer in a household.

### Activity: Support for the development of irrigation areas, farm management, and their functioning

In Niger, farmers' organizations are made up of producers who have common interests. The Water Users Associations (WUA). Several groups form a network that is coordinated by the association. The importance in the development of irrigation areas lies in the fact that it allows for the implementation of new crops and the diversification of agricultural activities. It also causes of insufficient development of certain regions, which leads to the creation of water conflicts. The Ministry of Agriculture and Hydraulics issued joint instructions on the organization of associations of producers for the establishment of an association of irrigators in each commune where a WUA exists.

### Activity: Support for the functioning of AUEGs and their management

As mentioned above, the cooperative ownership model is based on the principle of collective management of the water use. The AUEG is to be considered as a producer's organization that manages maintenance and management of the water tax.

## Activity 2.1.4: Development of operating contracts for cooperatives and AUEs

To ensure the sustainable operation of the decentralized agricultural extension services, it is proposed to sign operating contracts with cooperatives and AUEs. These contracts should include among other things:

- (i) set performance indicators;
- (ii) define the scope of activities to align targets and improve productivity to be managed sustainably; 
- (iii) define the expected results for each activity, including the responsibilities of the different farmer organizations and their members.

## Activity 2.1.5: Planning activities (AUEs)

The success of a given agricultural campaign depends on the planning carried out by cooperatives in planning the activities of the agricultural extension services. An exhaustive list of all activities involved in organizing agricultural campaigns will be developed, including:

- The calendar for each activity;
- The expected results from each activity.

In this planning of activities, the crop calendar is defined based on the climatic conditions (rainfall and usual droughts), abrupt ending of rainy seasons and other weather events. This information will be used with materialistic services to disseminate extension services in a timely manner for producer level. This information will make it possible to take advantage of the best time for each activity.

## Activity 2.1.2: Support to cooperatives in the acquisition of agricultural equipment

During the farming season, due to the lack of small equipment as a major difficulty in the implementation of extension services (harvesters, cutters, cassava, dates, ashrams, etc.), it is proposed that the purchase be made at the first stage of promoter development.

## Activity 2.3.1: Agrometric and agricultural input mechanisms

Agrometric monitoring is important in obtaining better yields for farmers. It will be proposed to cooperatives in identifying agri-culture needs at the start of the season, with the aim of empowering these cooperatives in identifying their own needs (NAMs) and those of their members whose collaboration will be necessary (NHMs). The NAMs will be defined by the National Agricultural Agency (CAMA). Office des Produits Agricoles et de la Sécurité Alimentaire (OPASA), etc.).

The project will make available the possibility to identify the types of inputs needed for the rapid integration of resident producers. These inputs are: (i) to provide technical and financial support; (ii) to micro-credit programs; (iii) to purchase agricultural inputs.

## Activity 2.3.2: Mechanism for agrometric monitoring, soil fertility and pest control

The project will provide financial support for the acquisition of equipment by producers during this first year.

**Page 81**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2 | PAGE 79 OF 95

## Activity 2.2.4 Promotion of composting using the organic waste from farmers and processors

To minimize the use of chemical fertilizers that deteriorate the quality of the soil in the medium and long term, as well as to improve the quality of the products, the project aims to promote organic composting by farmers and processors. The project will provide good raw materials, improvements, and conservers of soil fertility, as well as a composting machine that provides a composting mechanism that can be used in the case of composting and support for the promotion of organic composting among farmers and processors.

The groups will contribute to the promotion of organic composting by providing training on composting techniques and the use of composting machines. The number of small pits and heap composting sites will depend on the size of the site.

### 2.2.4.a Strengthen producer awareness about composting

### 2.2.4.b Smoke pits and compost heaps at each site for composting raw materials (animal droppings) needed by producers

### 2.2.4.c Raw materials for composting provided to producers

## Activity 2.3.1 Design, implementation and maintenance of irrigation infrastructure

Network maintenance is a prerequisite for proper water circulation and optimal irrigation performance.

These are preventive maintenance and curative maintenance works that will be carried out before the campaign and end, as the name suggests, it should be done every year. The curative maintenance takes place when there is an emergency or breakdown over. The management of the pit is an important element to ensure its proper functioning and must be determined by consensus with all stakeholders.

### To do so, the project will define a maintenance mechanism that considers the types of hydraulic infrastructure (pits, channels, etc.) and their respective maintenance periods.

#### 2.3.1.a Support the implementation of the automated water management system (AUB: automatic irrigation, water level, etc.)

#### 2.3.1.b Design a maintenance plan for hydraulic infrastructure

#### 2.3.1.c Develop an annual maintenance manual for the operation and maintenance of the irrigation network at the expense of the cooperatives (water levels, water flow rates, etc.) and the maintenance manual for irrigation networks.

#### 2.3.1.d Determine the amount of water to be used in each campaign by the cooperative and the volume of water, the depreciation of the hydraulic infrastructure, and the maintenance of the irrigation equipment.

#### 2.3.1.e Assist the Irrigation Water Users Association (IWUA) in the management of microfinance institutions and agricultural banks to facilitate the implementation of payments.

## Activity 2.3.2 Design, implementation and maintenance of solar pumping equipment

The project will use solar energy as a tool to reduce GHG emissions by reducing the consumption of diesel fuel for irrigation purposes while ensuring the sustainability of the investments, in particular those related to the installation of the electrical water pumping stations.

### To do so, the project will design and implement maintenance works. The project will contract young local technicians (local engineers) on the maintenance and repair of solar equipment as well as manuals for minor maintenance operations. The project will also provide training reports to ensure that competencies are maintained.

#### 2.3.2.a Develop manuals in local languages for the operation of solar equipment as well as for the maintenance and repair by producers.

#### 2.3.2.b Train young local technicians (local engineers) on the maintenance and repair of solar equipment, as well as provide them with manuals for minor maintenance operations to ensure that competencies are maintained and records kept.

#### 2.3.2.c Committee specializing in the operation and maintenance of solar equipment determining the amount of energy charged by producers and ensuring that training reports are ensured that competencies are maintained.

## Activity 2.3.3: Development of a program for continuous monitoring of the efficiency of the water supply and irrigation structures

The project will develop a close and permanent monitoring program of the physical investments made on the water supply and irrigation infrastructure, including networks, protection, irrigation, drainage, and other related works. This includes monitoring of channels, pumping stations, siphons, regulators for the operation of the pumps, and other components of the works and equipment installed.

This close and permanent monitoring will be ensured by a team of technicians from the Department of Agriculture. The latter will be tasked with carrying out the following activities: 2.3.3a Develop a disease monitoring program for the maintenance of structures and equipment; 2.3.3b Undertake a monitoring mission of the technical services delivered to farmers; 2.3.3c Provide financial support for the project for the missions required.

## Activity 2.4.1: Support for the implementation of business plans at the level of groups and cooperatives

Knowledge of entrepreneurial tools is necessary to ensure that the members of the groups and cooperatives are able to develop their own business plans and the linking of cooperatives with their target customers.

## Activity 2.4.2: Support for development of processing units based on the micro-processing unit technology

As the market garden products promoted under this project are seasonal, they are often in abundance but are frequently confronted with post-harvest losses because they are limited by the commercialization process and the lack of appropriate drying systems.

The introduction of improved drying systems has been proposed to preserve the quality of these products. This will provide additional income to producers. In rural areas where there is no access to markets, this will be a significant advantage for small-scale farmers who produce surplus goods at a price lower than what transport costs would allow them to sell their products.

The project will support the development of business plans adapted to new technologies and commercialization concepts, considering the market developed

## Activity 2.4.3: Support for groups and youth sales teams

Cereal products (rice, wheat) are imported at times during a period when production financial needs are high in the country. However, this period corresponds to that when the prices of these products on the international markets are low because they need to re-import them; the project aims to introduce micro-processing units for the processing and drying of cereal products which will be installed by the end of 2014 and will create jobs and increase the income of producers.

As market garden products promoted under this project are seasonal, they are often in abundance but are frequently confronted with post-harvest losses because they are limited by the commercialization process and the lack of appropriate drying systems.

## Activity: Strengthening knowledge on climate change and its variability in the context of local government officials

### 3.1.1 Improving high-resolution weather forecasts represents a challenge for building capacity to adapt to the climate, especially at the local level where there is limited access to locally to know the agro-climate and agro-ecological conditions that are critical for agricultural development in the climate. Given the limited technical capacity in the area of climate change adaptation, it is necessary to implement a project aimed at strengthening knowledge on climate change and its variability in the context of local government officials.

#### 3.1.1.1 Produce weekly, daily climate data and weather forecasts for direct local use

## Activity 3.2.2: Set up an on-lending mechanism for climate smart agricultural value chain financing through the loan of an agricultural loan facility with a maximum loan amount calculated at a rate of interest in contact other than the interest rate charged by the support regional or national financial institutions (the "Agriculture Loan Facility")

From a general observation, and despite the existence of a bank specialized in the agricultural sector, Nonga Agricultural Bank, this bank provides an innovative and sufficient offer that meets the needs of farmers for access to credit and medium-term credit to fund equipment and inputs necessary for the development of their activities on the value chain network. However, agriculture contributes only 10% of GDP and employs about 75% of Niger's working population. As of 31 January 2020, the total number of agricultural loans granted by Nonga Agricultural Bank was 489,600, with 30% being in favor of farmers, i.e., 15% of the Total Value Chain Development of the Strategic Plan for Agriculture and Food Security (SPAFS) of 2020 USD. From 2021 to 2024, the total amount allocated by Nonga Agricultural Bank for the implementation of the SPAFS project is $83 million. The agricultural public financing programs cover these needs.

Therefore it is also set up an on-lending mechanism with private financial institutions on the basis of a loan facility (the "Agriculture Loan Facility") in the form of an agricultural loan facility, which will be implemented through a 5-step stage, in the context of other projects, with the aim of providing access to credit and financial instruments (the "Agriculture Loan Facility"). For this purpose, it is proposed to put in place the Agricultural Loan Facility.

The technical control of the construction of the borrowings and the supervision of the equipment will be carried out by a consulting engineering office that carries out the work. The General Directorate of Rural Engineering (DGGR) or any other Ministry responsible for rural engineering will provide the General Directorate of Rural Engineering

## Activity: Monitoring of Project

### 3.4.2 Implementation of a Project Monitoring and Evaluation System

According to Nigeria's NDC, the monitoring evaluation are conducted through the Project Management Unit.

The project system for project activities will be put in place to ensure that the project is implemented effectively and efficiently with emphasis on the identification of strengths and weaknesses in order to inform informed decisions. The project management unit will be responsible for:

- Monitoring and evaluating the financial status of the project.
- Measuring progress against the overall output of the project.
- Disaggregated data collection and reporting.

The monitoring system will be designed to measure the rate of achievement of the project outputs, outcomes, results and targets as reflected in the Annual Work Plan. The system will also monitor the performance of the financial side of the project: (i) regular; and (ii) semi-annual reports on the status of the project.

## F: RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

### Risk assessment and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

#### Selected Risk Factor 1
**Category:** Currency risk
**Impact:** Medium
**Description:** All currency coming from GCF will be expressed in Euro.
**Mitigation:** The FX risk is hedged by converting FCFA to Euros, which is a natural hedge.

#### Selected Risk Factor 2
**Category:** Probability: Low
**Impact:** Medium
**Description:** Implementing a loan facility for local financial institutions. GCF will bear the credit risk of the local financial institutions.
**Mitigation:** The risk may be mitigated by selecting financial institutions based on BOAD's solid selection criteria, among others.

#### Selected Risk Factor 3
**Category:** Probability: Low
**Impact:** Medium
**Description:** Determining security conditions in the Project area. Cooperating with regional and community security services to set up an alert system in case of terrorist attacks.
**Mitigation:** Implement a joint security plan for the project site, including local methods of conflict management based on best practices.

#### Selected Risk Factor 4
**Category:** Probability: Low
**Impact:** Medium
