## Description
A project proposal focused on the installation and monitoring of solar panels, electric pumps, etc.

### Mitigation Measures
- All equipment will be fitted with the grids to ensure continuous monitoring of the installed equipment.
- The solar panels, the electric pumps, the California well have to bear the mark "Owned by Niger/CNIGRAN" with an identification number. If possible, the supplier must provide a maintenance manual, especially for the solar panels and electric pumps; a good experienced water system engineer will be hired to train the cooperatives' mobile phones.
- The GPS tracker installed on each equipment will allow the project team to track it in real-time from the office or even from the field. This technology has proven itself in the monitoring of large construction sites in the construction sector.

## Selected Risk Factor B
### Category: Probability | Impact
- Low | High

### Description
Lack of support from local administrative authorities (town hall and regional councils).

### Mitigation Measures
Obtain the support of local administrative authorities by involving them from the project design phase through meetings and joint visits to water points in each selected region, department, and commune.

## Selected Risk Factor B
### Category: Probability | Impact
- Low | Medium

### Description
Non availability lands for ANA's development.

### Mitigation Measures
Disseminate information for ANA’s land Governance; land voluntarily ceded by a landowner; land acquisition for ANA’s development, land securing, registration and distribution, land tenure security activities in the perimeters, land management, land tenure conflict resolution, etc. The project will also work with local authorities to ensure that all lands acquired are registered in the communes that it currently excludes from it because of "land problems"; land documents archiving; land audit, etc., and monitoring- evaluation of the implementation of Law 20 of Founding proposal. Land securing methodology for ANA’s development and its replication.

### Selected Risk Factor 13
**Category:** Financial Security  
**Impact:** Medium  
**Description:** The risk of non-compliance with BOAD's Financial Security Policy and procedures to contract financial flows, money laundering and terrorist financing (ML/FT). BOAD will conduct an assessment and a thorough ML/FT Due Diligence as part of the prevention and vigil against money laundering on any beneficiaries and counterparties before the start of the Project financing activities. This is a standard procedure for all projects.

In accordance with BOAD's Financial Security Policy and procedures relative to ML/FT, this due diligence will take into account: (1) an assessment of the risks and vulnerabilities of money laundering (ML), terrorist financing (TF), prohibited practices (PP) and integrity risk; (2) a review of the policies and procedures in place for the prevention and vigil against money laundering and terrorist financing consideration; (3) a summary of any risks and vulnerabilities identified; (4) an indication of severity for these risks and vulnerabilities, and (5) recommendations to mitigate these risks.

BOAD has a solid risk management framework to combat money laundering (ML), terrorist financing (TF), prohibited practices (PP) and integrity risk. BOAD's experience in the region is that financial institutions within the UEMOA region are less likely than banks managed ML/FT risks in the country. Similarly, BOAD lending portfolio to financial institutions within the UEMOA region is about USD 50 million, which represents a very small proportion of its total lending portfolio (USD 1 billion). BOAD has therefore decided to manage ML/FT risks in Kenya on its own based on its extensive lending experience and the robustness of its ML/FT risk management framework.

## Triggered Performance Measures

### E3.1 Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts
- **Potential impacts:** Risk of non-compliance with national standards
- **Mitigation measures:** Comply with the measures recommended by the sub-project E3Is.

### E3.2 National use of pesticides for surface pollution prevention
- **Potential impacts:** Surface and groundwater pollution
- **Mitigation measures:** Awareness raising among company staff and workers on the risks of chemical pollution of water resources; Rigid control of the local waste of the building site as well as of the base of the building site; Continuous monitoring of the quality of the water in the area surrounding the construction site; Equipment of the site-base with a place for the rest of the personnel; Continuous monitoring and control of the use of pesticides by the company staff and workers on the roof and ground level.

### E3.3 Community Health and Security
- **Potential impacts:** Damage to the health, safety and security of the population
- **Mitigation measures:** Equip all workers with personal protective equipment (boots, gloves, noise mask, helmet); Equip the construction site base/with a first aid unit equipped with basic necessities; Ensure that all workers are aware of the risks of accidents linked to the non-compliance of safety rules and regulations; Equip the construction site base/with a fire extinguisher; Ensure that all workers are aware on the risks of contamination by hazardous chemicals (BTEX and PAHs).

## There is currently within ONAH, an Environmental Unit directly attached to the General Management and led by an environmental officer. The unit will be responsible for the implementation of the ESF/ESMP. The main tasks are:

### Project The Management Unit (PMU) will coordinate the implementation of the ESF and act as an interface with the project team. The PMU will also provide technical support to the project team in terms of human, material and financial resources, within the framework of the implementation of the ESF/ESMP.

- The main tasks are:
  - To ensure that the project is implemented according to the approved plan.
  - To ensure that all activities are carried out in accordance with the environmental and social aspects for the work and interfere with other actors. These experts will coordinate the preparation and follow-up of the environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) and the environmental management plan (environment, agriculture, forestry, hydraulics, etc.). These experts do not have any autonomy in environmental and social issues.

### The BNEE will ensure the environmental and social monitoring (compliance control of works and environmental and social aspects for the work and interfere with other actors). At the local level, the BNEE will be supported by the DEESEE housed in the DREED. The monitoring carried out by the BNEE will be based on the ESIA and the Environmental Management Plan (EMP) and will provide institutional support to the NEEDS in this monitoring under the framework of a Memorandum of Understanding.

The BNEE will also ensure the follow-up of its reports to WBG for action.

## BOAD has established a grievance mechanism as per the Grievance Policy and Procedures Manual which is an independent mechanism through which individuals who have been harmed because of a project funded or implemented by BOAD can seek redress. The purpose of this mechanism is to provide a forum for addressing Social and Economic potential for scaling up to deal with non-compliance and grievance issues arising from projects financed, implemented or supported by BOAD. It also provides a means through which individuals who have been funded or implemented by BOAD. It is intended to establish an effective dialogue between those affected by the project and the implementing entity of the project to resolve the grievances (providing them for an application without seeking to attribute responsibility or fault to any of these parties.

### At WBG level, the grievance mechanism is coordinated and managed by the Compliance and Regulatory Division (CRD). All complaints received will be forwarded to the CRD. The CRD will then forward the complaint to the BORD, the implementing entity of this proposal, by mail, email, fax or telephone. The full address is given below.

Public consultations were organized at two levels: (i) the consultation of technical services at central and regional levels with representatives from the Ministry of Energy, Ministry of Agriculture, Ministry of Environment, Ministry of Land Tenure, Investment, Irrigation (ONAH), etc., the National Environment Assessment Office, the Ministry of Electricity, Directorates in charge of Microfinance Institutions, National Agriculture Bank (BAGRI), etc.; and (ii) the consultation of representatives from local communities at the village level. The consultations were held with representatives across the regions of Maradi, Tchadoua, Agadez, Zinder and Diffa Regional Governments, Representatives of the Districts of Maradi, Tchadoua, Agadez, Zinder and Diffa, Representatives of the Prefectures of Maradi, Diffa, Zinder, Tchadoua, Agadez, Zinder and Diffa, Environment, Hydrology, ESSA, environment offices of Agriculture, Livestock, Land Tenure, Irrigation, etc.

The plan of the Accredited Entity (AE) and EEIs to continue to engage the stakeholders throughout project implementation will be carried out in accordance with the following requirements: follow the requirements of the GCF Information Disclosure Policy and Environmental and Social Policy.

### G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The gender analysis is a tool that identifies the gaps between women and men that are aligned with the objectives of GCF's Gender Policy. Confirm a gender assessment and action plan exists describing the process used to identify these gaps and how they will be addressed. The gender assessment should include an analysis (how to address the vulnerability identified) of the gender assessment indicator if stakeholder consultations have taken place, the results of the gender assessment should be shared with all stakeholders involved in the project. Equal participation and benefits from funds investment: Key gender-related results to be expected from the project are that women will participate equally in the project activities (50% of women and men). The number of women implemented and expected outcomes will be achieved, monitored and evaluated. Provide the full gender assessment and action plan.

This project will be carried out in areas where there are no indigenous peoples.

In accordance with the GCF's gender policy, a gender analysis was conducted. The average age at first marriage for women is 16.3 years (50% of women aged between 20-49), compared to 68.5 years nationally. Women have an average life expectancy of 63.5 years nationally, 64.4 years for women and 60.8 years for men. In 2015, the participation rate of women in the labor market was 40.5% compared to 79.5% for men. The female literacy rate is 53%, compared to 78% nationally. Female school enrollment rose from 73% in 2006 to 84% in 2012, and progress in secondary school enrollment (female-to-male ratio of 66.8%) is better than the national average (59%). The female labor force participation rate for women aged between 15-64 years old was 37%, compared to 77% nationally. Women's economic activity rate is 20%, compared to 50% for men. The proportion of households headed by women and food security is 89%. The average number of children per woman in the region is 5.7, compared to 64 were economically active (compared to 90% of men). The proportion of households headed by women and food security is 89%.

Overall, gender analysis shows that women and young people are more vulnerable than men, particularly in terms of access to land, credit, care, education, etc. These inequalities persist despite the significant efforts of the Nigerian government to promote gender equality through the adoption of the National Policy on Gender Equality, the implementation of national gender policy and the adoption of Women's Day on 13 May. The weight of cultural and religious traditions and the low level of women's participation in decision-making processes are among the main factors that contribute to this situation.

Public consultations were held in the various areas of the project and involved all stakeholders, especially women, young


---
**Page 94**
---

people, and men (see ESMF public consultation report). The issues raised during the exchanges were considered in the analysis undertaken for the development of the action plan. They focused on: (i) the fear of a minority grabbing of the project; (ii) the method of land transfer; (iii) the criteria for making the developed plots available. These actors have been engaged to ensure that the gender action plan is fully integrated into the project design and implementation.

The gender action plan is designed to enhance women’s participation in the project through the improvement and diversification of the livelihoods of 127,615 people (including 50% women) in rural communities currently dependent on rain-fed agriculture in 5 regions of Nigeria. Many actions are planned in the gender action plan (GAP). The following is a summary of the key elements of the GAP:

R1: The capacities of vulnerable groups including women in terms of gender mainstreaming in the project are strengthened.

R2: Women’s access to resources and services, especially land, credit, extension services and inputs are strengthened.

R3: Women and men have equal access to decision-making bodies. R4: Women and men have equal access to information on the project and its activities.

R5: The gender action plan is set up in a participatory manner with women’s participation ensured at all levels of planning, implementation, monitoring and evaluation.

## BOAD WILL CONDUCT AN ANALYSIS OF THE PROCUREMENT FILES AND GIVE ITS NO-OBSTACLE ALMUTT DUE AS PART OF THE PREVENTION AND FIGHT AGAINST MONEY LAUNDERING. BOAD HAS DEVELOPED A FINANCIAL SECURITY POLICY AND PROCEDURES (2016) WHICH ARE AVAILABLE ON THE WEBSITE: https://www.boad.org/gestion-financiere/la-politique-de-securite-finance et le protocole de lutte contre le blanchiment de capitaux et le financement du territoire. <https://www.boad.org/groupement-banque-africaine-des-developpements> (BOAD) is committed to the prevention and fight against money laundering and terrorist financing in accordance with the general framework of the control system covering all of BOAD's activities relating to the prevention, surveillance and repression of money laundering and terrorist financing.

### BOAD FINANCIAL SECURITY POLICY IS INSPIRED BY THE INTERNATIONAL STANDARDS DEFINED BY THE UNITED NATIONS THROUGH ITS SPECIALIZED STRUCTURES. THE ORGANIZATION FOR ECONOMIC COOPERATION AND DEVELOPMENT (OECD) WHEREAS RECOMMENDATIONS ARE SET OUT IN THE OECD CONVENTION ON PREVENTING AND PUNITING MONEY LAUNDERING AND TERRORIST FINANCING OF 20 JULY 2015 ON THE FIGHT AGAINST MONEY LAUNDERING AND TERRORIST FINANCING IN THE MEMBER STATES OF THE WEST AFRICAN ECONOMIC COMMUNITY (WAEC).

The policy requires to disclose information about its clients' transactions to the relevant authorities in cases where international rules and local law require regulated financial institutions to do so, including cases of money laundering. Under this policy, BOAD is required to report suspicious transactions to the Central Bank of West African States (BCS) and also conducts administrative investigations into corruption, fraud, coercion, collisions, and inconvenient practices, and make use of its powers under the law.

BOAD integrates measures to combat illegal financial flows, the fight against money laundering and terrorist financing in the internal operations of the Bank Group. In the context of this project, concerning ALMUTT, the concerned sub-saharan country has been identified as a high-risk country for money laundering and terrorist financing. The BOAD will monitor Niger's financial interests, corruption, organized criminal activities, terrorism or drug trafficking; and forward to the Bank with any information that may indicate the illicit origin of assets or other illegal activities. In addition, the BOAD will monitor the project - notify the Bank without delay if it has known at any time of any information indicating the illicit origin of an asset or other illegal activity. The BOAD will also ensure that the project is not linked to any person or entity included on the lists established by the United Nations Security Council or its committees pursuant to Security Council resolutions, by the Central Bank of West African States (BCS) and/or by the Financial Intelligence Unit (FIU) of each country, as well as any other relative or complementary resolution and any act of implementation thereof in connection with the fight against the money laundering and terrorist financing.

The control procedure at Bank level is related to the justification of such resource disbursement. Indeed as soon as there is a first disbursement all the resource disbursed is not justified cent by cent through valid accounting documents, nor does it have any other relative or complementary resolution and act of implementation thereof in connection with the fight against money laundering and terrorist financing.

The AE does not have any intentions to distribute or disburse to beneficiaries, either directly or indirectly, cash, vouchers, commodities or other items of value.

### AUDIT

Audits will be undertaken in accordance with BOAD Guidelines for Financial Reporting and Auditing of Projects on annual basis. An independent external auditor will be recruited based on Terms of Reference acceptable to BOAD (not later than 30 days after the end of each financial year) and will be required to submit an audit report within a period not exceeding six months in accordance with international auditing standards. The local financial institutions relevant to the project will also be audited by the independent external auditor.

BOAD has all the right to provide observations, comments, and recommendations for improvements in accounting records, systems, controls, and compliance with financial covenants in the Financial Agreements. The cost of the audit will either be borne by BOAD or the Borrower. BOAD will ensure IFRS standard-compliant due diligence process including anti-money laundering.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP176

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### Strengths

- The project enhances climate resilience of agricultural production systems and related value chains.
- Climate-smart approaches to increase production and food security as well as reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions.

### Points of caution

- The project will be implemented in select hydro-agriculture developments (known as the “Agricultural Development Areas” or ADA) and has high potential for scaling up. Additional public investment is required to implement and disseminate the innovative mechanisms and improved practices for the project.
- Transfer of technology and managerial capacity are crucial for successful implementation and maintenance of the facilities. Capacity building activities need to take into account the specific needs of the cooperatives in terms of capacity of farmers’ cooperatives for successful operation and maintenance of the facilities.

### Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

#### Project background

- Agriculture is the main source of income and livelihoods for 87 per cent of the population of Niger. Food insecurity, poverty and malnutrition are rising in Niger because of the


---
**Page 105**
---

negative effects of climate change on the agricultural sector. About 12 per cent of the rural population are food insecure and 29 per cent are at risk. Most agriculture in Niger is rain-fed, and the Government of Niger is making its utmost efforts to expand irrigated agriculture and climate-resilient practices with the aim of increasing production and productivity and reducing food insecurity.

### Funding source (Euro)
- GCF: 30,138,772
- Government of Niger: 6,519,199
- Banque Ouest Africaine de Développement (BOAD): 9,258,444

### Funding uses
- Component I: Sustainable development of irrigated agriculture in areas vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change and greenhouse gas emissions: 31,401,760 (69%)
- Component II: Capacity-building of stakeholders' technical, organizational and institutional capacities for promoting climate-resilient agricultural practices: 9,370,757 (21%)
- Project management costs: 1,397,295 (6%)

### Total
45,548,415

**Page 106**

# Component-by-component analysis

## Component 1: Sustainable development and rehabilitation of perimeters vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change and reducing greenhouse gas emissions (total cost: EUR 30,403,768; GCF cost: EUR 25,924,114)

### 1.1 Water availability

Water scarcity is a result of rainfall and lowering food insecurity. There is a need to increase the irrigation areas in Niger to achieve food security. Component 1 aims to develop new irrigation areas as well as rehabilitate existing ones. The project will be implemented through Agro-Industrie (AIA) (hydro-agriculture development). The existing AIA have been developed in Niger with non-pumping wells, which are powered by diesel engines and used for irrigation, and users draw groundwater from the fossil fuel-powered electricity network using pumping systems to water their crops. This project will introduce two new types of irrigation: solar energy-based drip irrigation and solar energy-based sprinkler irrigation.

The project interventions have been designed in 23 districts covering an area of approximately 105,000 ha. The project will be implemented in 23 zones covering 1,001 ha and rehabilitating the two existing AIAAs, spanning an area of 759 ha. In addition to these interventions, the project will also implement a number of measures aimed at enhancing water use efficiency thus allowing additional areas to be brought under irrigation.

Furthermore, the AIAAs will be developed along with other climate-resilient practices to avoid the negative effects of climate change on agricultural production and food security. The adoption of drip irrigation will provide total control over the water flow while the farming plots will be protected by a system of windbreaks, which will also protect crops from pests and diseases and ensure better soil conservation measures.

The component includes installation of solar energy-based sprinkler irrigation units from bore holes replacing the traditional diesel generators and thus contributing to greenhouse gas emissions reductions. Complete sets of installation kits will be provided, with each being customizable to suit the needs of farmers. The project will also provide 10 additional water pumps for irrigation services but an additional 37 solar pumping systems will be installed to provide drinking water.

The activities will capitalize on the institutional mechanisms developed by the Government of Niger for AIAAs such as the demarcation of the perimeter (i.e., creating new or reporting existing) and the creation of farmers' groups, which are organized into cooperatives and manage water resources. Inclusion of poor people, groups that are most vulnerable to climate change effects, will be ensured through the use of a comprehensive set of eligibility criteria for selecting participating farmers/producing units.

This component comprehensively addresses the challenges of developing and expanding irrigated areas in Niger while contributing to the achievement of the project's overall objective: Component 2: Support of the sustainable increase in agricultural productivity and income of farmers' groups and cooperatives in irrigated areas on developed and rehabilitated perimeters (total cost: EUR 30,403,768; GCF cost: EUR 25,924,114).

Under this component, farmers' cooperatives will be formed to delineate the perimeters and optimal development of irrigated areas. Plot allocation to beneficiaries will be undertaken based on criteria agreed by the cooperative members. The project will also provide training for beneficiaries so that they can establish and manage cooperatives, and operations will be undertaken in collaboration with local authorities.

Improved crop production technologies will be promoted to increase productivity and income of farming communities. Climate-resilient practices will be adopted for crops selected


---
**Page 107**
---

## Assessment of performance against investment criteria

### Impact potential

As the proposed cross-cutting is expected to bring both adaptation and mitigation benefits, the number of people expected to directly benefit from the project activities is 121,657 (30,849 producers' households on the newly irrigated areas; 50,608 producers' households on the rehabilitated AMAs (24,415 beneficiaries of the line of credit for scaling up through the private sector; and 39,200 state workers who would benefit from capacity-building).


---
**Page 108**
---

activities (10%), and people directly acquiring agricultural products from project sites to support their food security (15.3%).  

24. The two largest sets of beneficiaries above correspond to project component 1, the rehabilitation and development of AMAs. The project aims to rehabilitate 749 ha of agricultural land and develop 600 ha of new AMA in the West African region. This will substantially increase the availability of food both among producers and at the national level.

25. Indirect beneficiaries total 1 million, based on population estimates in the villages benefiting from project activities. These indirect benefits are expected to result from dissemination of good agricultural practices through guides and dissemination of agrometeorological information.

26. On the mitigation side, the proposed project is estimated to reduce or avoid 227,551 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) over its lifespan, which corresponds to an annual figure of 38,092 tonnes CO2e per year. The reduction impact figures are very high, while the mitigation impact figures are relatively modest; they still represent meaningful expected impacts on the mitigation side of the project.

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential

Scale: Medium to high

28. As noted in the proposed document, the proposed project would be the first of its kind on Africa’s continent and would serve as a model for scaling up the project across Nigeria and at least in part on its neutral or positive impact on water resources; the analysis as provided indicates that scaling up is feasible. The analysis also indicates that there are no significant adverse impacts uprating of the project in the future is feasible and here the risk of overexploitation of the resource and therefore the need for a careful monitoring system. The World Bank has stated that the project could be scaled up across the West African region and beyond, but this potential needs to be assessed more thoroughly in the context of a future project.

29. In terms of paradigm shift on agricultural development has not yet extended into Niger. The technical institutions involved in the project have previously piloted AMA development activities from thermal power generators or electrical grids. This is an important step forward and will provide a model for other innovative in the context of AMAs. Potential beneficiaries of the irrigation areas to be developed lack the technical capacity and institutional support needed to manage these resources, which will receive the institutional and technical capacity-building needed. The planned interventions are based on the assumption that this capacity can be built up over time.

30. In terms of paradigm shift on the financing side, it is expected that GCF’s interventions will significantly reduce interest rates to small farmer borrowers from 25-30 per cent to around 7 or even less. This would make agricultural loans more affordable and therefore become more attractive once the project has ended, thus stimulating more loan applications and investments in agriculture.

31. By demonstrating the potential of the interventions and making financing more affordable, future cooperatives and agricultural groups should be more willing to take out both local and international loans. The project will also provide technical assistance from farms by the ministry responsible for agriculture and also supported by this project’s loan facility or a similar one in the future. This would help to support agricultural groups and cooperatives to transition to other sources of private financing.

### 3.3 Sustainable development potential

Scale: Medium to high


---
**Page 109**
---

28. Statutory development potential is particularly strong regarding livelihoods and economic co-benefits. The project is expected to provide stable incomes for beneficiaries, especially young people who often emigrate elsewhere due to lack of economic opportunity. The project will therefore limit to some extent the phenomenon of rural exodus seen in the project area.

29. Other notable co-benefits include a significant estimated improvement in production and yields (10–35 per cent) for smallholder farmers, increased access to irrigation resources coming from agroforestry income, water savings, and clean energy savings, which can represent 40 per cent of the entire production income; and a significant economic boost to agricultural cooperatives and groups.

### 3.4 Needs of the recipient
**Scale: High**

30. As noted in the project documents and in other GCF projects in the country, Niger is a least developed country and faces extraordinary challenges in ensuring its development aligns with climate-resilient, low-emission development pathways. Agriculture is the main source of income for rural households and is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts and threatened by the adverse effects of climate change, including irregular rainfall, shorter rainy seasons, and increased frequency of extreme weather events such as droughts and flooding.

31. At the local level, project beneficiaries are vulnerable farmers and their groups and cooperatives. These farmers tend to be small producers of wheat, maize, rice and vegetables. Soil productivity is low due to soil degradation and erosion caused by climate change, which increases climate vulnerability of producers and limit their ability to adapt to climate-related changes.

### 3.5 Country ownership
**Scale: High**

32. The project will contribute to the implementation of the 2017–2020 Four-Year Contract between Niger State and the project's executing entity (NE), Niger Office of Hydro-Agricultural Development (ONHA). Niger considers ONHA as central to its AHA strategy, which is aimed at increasing agricultural production by planting plans 4,000 ha of new hydro-agricultural developments and 2,000 ha of rehabilitated hydro-agricultural developments.

33. This project is consistent with Niger's nationally determined contributions (NDCs). The development of AHAs with climate-resilient techniques and a solar water pumping system is in line with the co-benefits of adaptation and mitigation advocated by the nationally determined contributions, which also place national priorities for the agriculture, forestry and other land use (AFOLU) sector.

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness
**Scale: Medium to high**

34. The profitability of the developed irrigation areas (see the funding proposal: feasibility study, and economic and financial analysis) shows that producers, in the short term, will be able to benefit from the project's co-benefits.

35. The project aims to promote solar energy instead of thermal generators for water pumping. The California irrigation system and the drip irrigation systems are promoted in all of the proposed areas. The use of solar-powered irrigation systems is expected to reduce water loss by 70 per cent compared with about 56 per cent for open-channel systems. In the latter case, the losses are high, and the maintenance costs are significant. To ensure the quality and performance of

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

**30. Project background:** The project aims to develop AIMA with climate-smart agriculture techniques in order to mitigate the adverse effects of climate change, mitigation of GHG emissions from production yields and incomes of farmers' groups and cooperatives. The project has three components: (i) sustainable land rehabilitation and reforestation; (ii) the adoption of climate-effective change; (iii) support for the development of new and rehabilitated perimeters; and (iv) developing stakeholders technical capacity to ensure the sustainability of the project.

**31. Environmental and social risk category:** The project is classified as category II, because it is not expected to negatively affect forest resources or indigenous populations. The AE also determined that the project will not have a significant adverse environmental impact. Project activities will not take place in disputed areas, or areas sensitive to land issues or on natural resource reserves, and the project does not cross the environment and social (E&S) risk classification level.

**32. Safeguard instruments:** The AE's environmental and social performance standards are applied to all components of the project. The ESS provides a framework for project design which requires any development activity that could potentially harm the environment to go through an environmental and social assessment. For AIMA, the AE has prepared an ESMP. An integrated pest and pesticide management plan (IPMPM) will be developed to ensure the rational use of chemical products such as pesticides and agrochemicals when implementing the activities.

**33. Compliance with the GCF environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards:** Paragraphs 46-47 below summarize the project's compliance with the GCF ESS standards (ESF, L&H).

**ESS 1: Assessment and management of E&S risks and impacts:** The ESMP provides an overview of the potential environmental and social risks associated with each activity selected under the project (i.e. 23 new sites as well as rehabilitation of four old sites) constitutes the physical framework of a sub-project that will be the subject of an environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA). The ESIA will identify, assess and mitigate the risks to be assessed from an environmental and social perspective in accordance with the country's regulations. The ESMP will also include a description of the project management plan including approval of the ESIA, and acquisition of a certificate of environmental compliance.

**ESS 2: Labour and working conditions:** The AE has determined that the risk factors associated with the project are related to child labour, forced recruitment for employment, child employment and forced labour. Discrimination and marginalization risk factors relate to Ngoro company's pattern of hiring only able-bodied men and young men who can perform


---
**Page 111**
---

difficult to work. Women and people with disabilities are thus routinely considered unfit and are seldom hired. The AE has made a commitment to complying with Nigeria’s Labour Code, which prohibits forced or compulsory labour. To further mitigate these risks, the AE will establish an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) for: addressing the risk of child labour; and for respecting provisions of the Labour Code and the International Labour Organization Conventions.

### ES 5 - Resource efficiency and pollution prevention
Identified potential risks and impacts include pressure on ground water, surface groundwater pollution, change in soil structure, and loss of biodiversity. The AE has established a mitigation strategy to address these issues in the ESMP via mitigation/avoidance and enhancement measures during both the construction and operational phases. The project will also establish an environmental management system (EMS) as part of every operation, including an environmentally friendly alternative to thermal energy. As such, there is also an IPPM to guide the management of pests and use of pesticides and agro-chemicals. Each of the potential risks and impacts has been addressed in the ESMP. An Environmental and Social Management Plan will be developed in accordance with the E&S performance standards and guidelines. The project will establish a monitoring system that will corroborate with the level of impacts and risks as identified in the field.

### ES 6 - Community health, safety and security
The potential risks and negative impacts include inadequate maintenance of technical equipment such as lifts, technical equipment and inadequate maintenance of technical equipment, a range of health and safety issues related to construction and operational phases. The project will also establish an environmental management system (EMS) as part of every operation, including an environmentally friendly alternative to thermal energy. As such, there is also an IPPM to guide the management of pests and use of pesticides and agro-chemicals. Each of the potential risks and impacts has been addressed in the ESMP. An Environmental and Social Management Plan will be developed in accordance with the E&S performance standards and guidelines. The project will establish a monitoring system that will corroborate with the level of impacts and risks as identified in the field.

### ES 7 - Land acquisition
The land acquisition component is not included in the funding proposal, but the AE has determined that ES5 is not applicable under the project, because it will not involve involuntary resettlement of populations. Land acquisition will be on a voluntary (e.g., donation) basis and will be carried out by the project company with the consent of all participants. The project has likewise developed a “land securing methodology” for developing and implementing land acquisition plans. This methodology is based on the principle that land will be part of the process of land title establishment, which will be done in a participatory manner.

### ES 8 - Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources
The ESMP includes descriptions of the biophysical characteristics of each of the five regions where the project will be implemented. The large water supplies in the dry or semi-arid areas of the project may create local microclimates that are conducive to the growth of invasive species, which could spread into the wetter areas downstream along drainage canals. The project could result in loss of vegetation cover, land degradation and soil erosion. To mitigate these risks, the ESMP includes a series of measures to avoid and mitigate these risks by briefly listed in the ESMP, including requirements such as limiting tree-felling, paving tree-felling trees, redistributing cut wood to communal areas for use as fuelwood, and planting new trees on cleared land. The project has committed to “offsetting losses of cash crops and trees with economic value.” Regarding distribution of benefits from the project, the ESMP includes a commitment that the project will include marking out the rights-of-way to avoid unnecessary overflow, especially when opening

### ESS 7: Indigenous peoples and the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy

The AE has confirmed that the project sites are not in the nomadic Tuareg area, and any herders who could be in the project area will be consulted by the PMU. The ESMSF will also consult with indigenous people if any indigenous people are found in the project area, taking into consideration the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy. This consultation will be done in accordance with the relevant national laws and regulations. Prior and informed consent will be sought before any subject is approved, in accordance with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy.

### ESS 8: Cultural heritage

The project activities may include "site development work (i.e., clearing, parceling, construction of physical structures, etc.)" and "opening of site access roads." Before carrying out these activities, the PMU will consult with relevant authorities to determine whether any cultural or historical items are present. If physical cultural items occur here, this will be reported to the relevant government authority and the AE for approval.

### Implementation arrangements

The project will establish a project management unit (PMU) to coordinate the implementation of the environmental and social management measures. The PMU will also develop an environmental and social management plan that will be established at the PMU to implement and monitor those measures in close coordination with relevant authorities, stakeholders, and communities. Environmental and social management plans will be undertaken by contractors and service providers who will be required to have environment, health and safety permits.

### Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure

Public consultations were undertaken with various categories of actors. These included consultations with technical services and representatives from the Ministry of Environment and Forestry, representatives from community communities at the local level, and two national workshops to validate draft study reports. Technical consultations included meetings with representatives from the various government ministries and agencies involved in the project (e.g., the Ministry of Environment and Forestry, Agadez, Diffa, Maradi, Tahoua and Zinder). Key concerns and stakeholders' suggestions and recommendations were recorded during these consultations. In addition to the preparation of the ESIA for each of the subprojects, a stakeholder engagement plan to be approved by the PMU will also be prepared.

### Grievance redress mechanism

The ESMSF provides for management of grievances first at the local level, then elevated to the level of the AE or the GCF if local resolution is not possible. If this level fails to resolve a grievance, it can be escalated to the national level. The process will depend only on the populations' general preference for conciliation with customary leaders (village and canton chiefs) or through the formal justice system. The PMU will ensure that all stakeholders are community participating in a subproject. The public will be informed about the existence of the bank, and it will be made available to the public at any time.

### Gender policy

The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.

The gender assessment indicates the existence of an enabling environment for promoting gender equality in Niger. The country has a national gender policy, a constitution


---
**Page 113**
---

laws that could contribute to gender equality. In addition to the legislative framework, Niger has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW). The institutional framework consists of a national ministry for the promotion of women and children’s rights, as well as several sectoral ministries responsible for specific issues and focal points within sectoral ministries. Other institutional mechanisms include the directorate for the education of girls in schools, and the national observatory for gender promotion that monitors gender equity and equality issues. Nonetheless, some legal codes still present barriers for women to access justice and participate fully in public life. Women are also required to follow professions and open bank accounts. The gender assessment discusses the established norms (customary laws) and practices that affect women’s ability to access resources and their right to equal pay. Social norms still govern gender issues and men are viewed as both breadwinners and decision-makers.

The gender assessment is based on a review of literature available to the AE. The AE has not yet undertaken stakeholder discussions and has not solicited inputs from stakeholders during the project preparation phase. The analysis focuses on the institutional level, with limited information on areas where the project could have an impact. However, the Ministry of Environment and Water Resources has committed to carry out further site-specific gender analyses involving relevant and various stakeholders.

The gender assessment provided discussion gender-related matters including on employment, education and access to information among others. Women are more likely to be victims of violence than men in Niger. The project will not have a direct impact as there are mechanisms for preventing gender-based violence and providing remedial actions, including the national observatory for gender promotion that monitors gender equity and equality issues.

The project mechanism that will be available to stakeholders who have concerns on the project is described in the environmental and social management framework, although the gender component of this framework has not yet been developed. The project will provide training to both women and men, given the prevailing cultural/traditional norms and practices in Niger.

Women represent 70% or more of the agricultural workforce in the agricultural sector. In this sector women face low productivity levels and encounter barriers such as limited access to manual labour, irrigation water, finance and fertilizers. Women also have less land than men and are less likely to own land; access to land is mainly through inheritance and is generally reserved for men. This turn negatively on their ability to participate in the agricultural sector. Additionally, female-headed households are less resilient than male-headed households and they are more food insecure during times of drought or other shocks. The project will provide training to women on how to improve productivity levels.

The AE has provided a gender level action plan that includes activities, performance indicators, budgetary allocations and monitoring and evaluation practices. Budgetary allocations are included in the action plan for most activities while for others financial resources for implementation have not been incorporated into the project plan yet. The action plan is expected to underpin additional gender analysis at 25 project sites. The analyses are expected to address the gap seen in stakeholder consultations, identify gender-related issues and carry out a baseline survey prior to commencing implementation of the project.

Activities listed in the gender action plan reflect opportunities presented by the project to provide training on climate-smart agriculture, access to credit, land ownership, climate information, and participation in training on climate-smart agriculture and decision-

### 4.3 Risks

#### 4.3.1 Overall project assessment (medium risk)

The funding proposal aims to increase agricultural production by improving crop yields through the strengthening beneficiaries' technological and financial capacities. GCF is requested to provide a grant of EUR 24.2 million and a loan of EUR 5.9 million. The borrowers of the GCF grant are the Government of Niger, while the borrower of the loan facility is the AIF.

Niger is a lower middle-income country. The project includes activities with revenue-generating potential that will be financed by GCF grants, an AIF loan and the AIF's sovereign loan. The AIF is providing a sovereign loan of EUR 9.2 million and the Government of Niger is waiving taxes on the proceeds from these revenues to support the overall bankability of the project while reducing the debt burden of the country.

#### 4.3.2 Accessing/entry/executing entity capability to execute the current project (medium risk)

BOAD, as a direct access AIF, has been operating in the West African region for 40 years and has experience in financing climate change projects in eight countries of the West African Economic Community (WAEC).

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP176

## Proposal name:
Hydro-agricultural development with smart agriculture practices resilient to climate change in Niger

## Accredited entity:
Banque Ouest Africaine de Développement (BOAD)

## Country/ies:
Niger

## Project/programme size:
Medium

### I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

#### 1.1 Impact potential
**Scale: High**

- This funding proposal for a Medium-sized Cross-cutting project, in environmental and social safeguards Category B, is submitted by the Republic of Niger (otherwise known as Niger) to the Green Climate Fund (GCF). The total value of this funding proposal is EUR 45.54 million, including a EUR 30.18 million Green Climate Fund (GCF) grant contribution, which forms two per cent of the total cost. The remaining EUR 15.4 million, or 34 per cent of the total cost, will be provided by the Government of Niger (EUR 16.16 million), and concessional loan finance from BOAD – the Banque Ouest Africaine de Développement.

- The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) assesses the impact potential of this cross-cutting project as HIGH, both including mitigation and adaptation aspects. In the past three decades, Niger has experienced a significant increase in climate variability and change. Flooding in Niger, with the five project regions of Tadoussé, Agadez, Maradi, Diffa and Zinder particularly vulnerable to flooding, is one of the most serious impacts of climate change. The High Amalgamated Developments (Aménagements Hydros-Agricoles or "AHAs"), vital to food security through irrigated agriculture in Niger, are also highly vulnerable to flooding. Increased temperatures combined with an overall declining trend in rainfall have led to more frequent and severe drought periods, which means it is increasingly important to expand the area under irrigation.

- The low emissions, climate-resilient development pathway for this expansion involves using herbicide-resistant crops, drip irrigation systems, and solar-powered pumping systems for water energy efficiency, powered by renewable energy technology.

- This project will contribute significantly to increasing food production through strengthening the resilience of populations to the adverse effects of climate change, through three components of work:

  - Component One: Expanding the expansion and climate-proofing of the AHAs against silation (caused by flooding intensified by climate change) in sites with untagged groundwater.
    - This will be achieved by converting existing irrigation systems from diesel-powered pumps to solar-PV powered pumps (instead of high emissions diesel-powered pumps). An area of 749 hectares of AHAs will be rehabilitated, and 1,000 new hectares of irrigated plots in the Sahel region will be established. This component also includes soil erosion enhancements and channels for drainage and aquifer recharge, and structures against erosion.


---
**Page 122**
---

# Mitigation impact: The cross-cutting project is expected to have a modest but significant impact through GHG emissions avoided as a result of the 23 newly established and expanded irrigation systems in the project area, which are more energy-efficient than existing systems, in contrast to a "without-project" scenario in which irrigation schemes would have continued to expand and become less efficient. The project is expected to avoid approximately 105,784 metric tons of CO₂-equivalent (i.e., 1,117,342 GHG units), per year over the 25-year lifetime of the project.

The basis for using LCM methodology ASM 4.6 and the calculation of the baseline is based on 2012 agro-ecological conditions in the project area, which involve equipping 10% of the PV systems with energy meters. Responsibility for monitoring of the energy generation and avoided emissions will be shared between the Ministry of Agriculture (MOA) and the National Council for Sustainable Development (CNDS), with collection of data by the MOA. (National Office of the Environment, Climate Change, Water Resources, Forestry, and Management of Man and Workrooms).

The common practice in Niger of using groundwater pumps to extract water from boreholes is a major source of CO₂, NOx, and particulate matter, and causes air pollution.

The independent TAP welcomes the shift through the project's investment to a clean and renewable energy source for irrigation. The project will also contribute to sustainable groundwater extraction, in line with the Ministry of Agriculture’s Strategy for small-scale irrigation systems. This is an important step toward reducing the country’s reliance on diesel fuel as an alternative of locking into a new generation of high-emission infrastructure, since diesel pumps have a lifetime of 10 years and are not energy-efficient.

The project also intends to help farmers in Niger transition from using diesel-powered pumps to solar-powered farming households with access to low-energy emission for purposes such as mobile phone charging, charging lighting systems and other portable battery-based appliances. The project also intends to help the Ministry of Agriculture (MOA) to develop a financing mechanism that will allow farmers to finance, though this is not without its challenges [see Paradigm shift section].

The independent TAP welcomes the shift from diesel-powered pumps to solar-powered renewable energy sources for groundwater extraction borehole pumps is wholly positive with respect to mitigation impact. Such a shift can pose the risk of potential maladaptation. Once the upfront capital is invested in the solar-powered pump, there will be no need to purchase fuel (i.e., diesel) and once the cost of diesel fuel is removed, and if this is combined with inadequate water governance mechanisms, there is potential for over-extraction of groundwater - see section below on "Risks of


---
**Page 123**
---

# Adaptation Impact

The project will help agricultural communities in the five most vulnerable regions of Niger to adapt to the negative effects of climate change - reducing their vulnerability and increasing their resilience.

### Project Benefits

- **Restoration of existing dams:** This will increase water availability for irrigation (drought resistance) and reduce sedimentation, especially where irrigation is unavailable or inadequate.
- **New irrigation systems:** These will be installed in areas where irrigation was previously impossible. The project also helps these communities adapt to a growing frequency of intense rainfall events - as shown by trends recorded in recent decades.

### Project Components

1. **Restoration of existing dams:**
   - This will increase water availability for irrigation (drought resistance) and reduce sedimentation, especially where irrigation is unavailable or inadequate.
2. **New irrigation systems:** These will be installed in areas where irrigation was previously impossible. The project also helps these communities adapt to a growing frequency of intense rainfall events - as shown by trends recorded in recent decades.

### Expected Benefits

- 123,615 people made up of:
  - (i) 54,086 farmers benefiting from the construction of new dams;
  - (ii) 49,199 people in the Afars to be rehabilitated;
  - (iii) 12,320 beneficiaries of the line of credit for private financial sector for small-scale irrigation systems.

### Risk of Maladaptation

A significant risk in groundwater-based projects in general is that the increased use of solar PV technology for pumping (removing the former constraint of cost), combined with a lack of proper management and monitoring, can lead to overextraction leading to a groundwater deficit - when water is pumped out faster than the rate at which it replenishes. This can result in drying up of wells, reducing the wellpoint unusable, or necessitating deeper "mining" of water that risks contamination by nitrates. As the water table drops, other wells in the area dry up, as do springs and ponds.


---
**Page 124**
---

harmful effects on biodiversity and human health. In many regions of the world, multiple poorly sited wetlands have also caused land subsidence, where the soil around the depleted aquifer collapses, compacts and dries, damaging assets and livelihoods.

13. Following questions posed to the Accreditation Entity (AE) why this risk was not highlighted in the Feasibility Study, the independent TAP noted that, measured, both on the process followed for selecting sites and determining extraction rates, and on the strong water governance in Niger, there is no reason to expect that such a risk would be encountered. In the feasibility study, with thresholds used to ensure that only areas where remote sensing has indicated that groundwater levels are at least 10 meters below the surface were selected, all groundwater analyses for the Iullemeden and Chad Basin Aquifer Systems, allowing for consultation of the results in the TAP report (see Annex A.2) and the “groundwater analysis” figures shown in Annex A.28. The independent TAP notes the conclusion in the Feasibility Study that "the project will exist on acceptable level of pressure that is within the aquifer's recharge capacity."

13. In response to questions from the independent TAP, the AE also highlighted that pumping rates are limited by the boreholes' capacities and not by the groundwater levels controlled by relay timers in the pumping systems. The new boreholes will be fitted with piezometers, to enable proactive and ongoing monitoring of groundwater pressure, to feed into water management plans, and to ensure that any over-extraction is detected early. This will also establish or strengthen and capacitate Irrigation Water Users Associations (IWAs), which are responsible for the operation and maintenance of the boreholes. The IWAs will be required to specify the constraints on pumping, and enforce sanctions if these are exceeded. The AE provided additional information in response to questions from the independent TAP regarding responsibilities of each Irrigation Water User Association and of ANAH, as set out in the operating contract between the parties.

1.2 Paradigm shift potential

The paradigm shift involved in the proposed project is rated as MEDIUM, with potential to increase the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change and drought hazards that helps agrarian community adapt. The project contributes towards the creation of new business activities and local markets for the production of the renewable and climate-resilient HIDs.

14. The project is well designed to support new approaches in irrigation and agriculture, incorporating a range of technologies and practices that will help smallholder farmers adapt to drought and climate change hazards that helps agrarian community adapt. The project contributes towards the creation of new business activities and local markets for the production of the renewable and climate-resilient HIDs.

15. The extent to which the project is able to bring about a real paradigm shift will depend on whether the technologies and practices are adopted by smallholder farmers in the target regions, and scaled up nationally. The groundwater analyses summarized in the feasibility study indicate that Niger has large and widespread untapped groundwater potential, where irrigation will be an important component of adaptation strategies (see the PANA). In the absence of massive additional aid devoted to the Government, beyond the


---
**Page 125**
---

current or parallel initiatives, the cost of realizing full-scale sustainable utilization of groundwater resources will need to be borne, at least in part, by loans from local financial institutions to producer associations, made possible with the costs of renewable energy technology.

16. Scaling up climate-resilient irrigated agriculture through crowding in the private sector in the form of local commercial finance institutions (as well as companies involved in agri-processing) will be a key challenge. The GCF is aware that there are significant due diligence impacts of the proposed loan finance element of the project. Through the EUR 8 million Agricultural Finance Facility (AFF), the GCF has already provided technical assistance and due diligence support to the national agricultural bank BAGRAH and commercial local financial institutions (LFIs) in order to help them develop a lending model for climate-resilient irrigation loans that are more competitive than currently available on the market to aggregated producer groups who can provide a collective collateral. The component may also include a guarantee fund. At present, however, the loan finance element of the project is not yet fully defined and there are no guarantees or annexes, meaning that fundamental information on the market potential, in particular uptake and scalability, remains unclear.

17. In response to questions raised from this the independent TAP, the AE indicated that the loan-financing component was added at a late stage, following interactions with the GCF, and therefore was not as fully developed as the other parts of the project. Nevertheless, the AE considered that the proposed loan finance element could be an important mechanism for work through co-operatives, which would enable it to reach farmers normally not considered creditworthy. One bank has already expressed interest in participating.

18. The independent TAP notes that the loan element of the project has been as thoroughly designed as the grant element, which had a full feasibility study including detailed groundwater studies, crop yield projections, market analysis, financial models, balance sheets, land governance issues, and crop yield projections. By contrast, the independent TAP has not seen any such information for the proposed loan finance component, with no funding mechanism and guarantee fund. A number of areas remain unclear, for example:

(a) the anticipated role of additional private sector investment, including the specifics of blending with GCF funds;

(b) the likely size of the market with cooperative borrowers for specific financial products and the terms which would be appropriate;

(c) the uncertainties around how much time are built into a financial model analysing potential loan performance;

(d) the exit strategy for the loan facility and how future loans without the GCF subsidy would be financed; and

(e) how linkages will be established with the closely related GCF SAP 012, approved at B.24.

19. The independent TAP furthermore remains concerned about the capacity of the targeted producer associations to absorb the costs associated with the project and their ability to repay loans, whilst also covering the costs of operations and maintenance post-project. This could undermine, rather than strengthen private sector involvement, if the failure rate were high or if there was a lack of financial management capacity within the associations as a whole.

20. Notwithstanding this risk, enhancing the bankability and financial management capacity of village savings and loans schemes, as well as of water user and producer associations would seem like a logical first step to take prior encouraging them to take on the risk of commercial debt. This is in line with the GCF’s approach towards supporting smallholder agriculture but fails to analyze in detail and provide clear steps how these barriers can be overcome. In this

## Sustainable development potential

The project’s sustainable development potential overall is rated as MEDIUM to HIGH. It has a rate of high benefit for the rural population and the environment in terms of achieving full participation in decisions on economic opportunities and decision-making related to the project.

The FP is accompanied by a solid Gender Analysis and Action Plan, but there seems to be a gap between the gender analysis and the action plan. The gender analysis is very good, but the plan that is not fully addressed by innovative thinking in the design of the project activities.


### Environmental co-benefits

1. The project’s actions to combat erosion, flooding and silting up of irrigated plots will include planting of trees and shrubs as windbreaks, plant species, and the practice of Farmer Managed Natural Regeneration and agroforestry will be beneficial to the restoration and maintenance of biodiversity in terms of soil structure, water retention, and micro-organisms.
2. The climate-smart agriculture methods that will be promoted as part of the project will improve soil quality and productivity, and facilitate carbon storage in the soil.


### Social co-benefits

The project has a number of important social co-benefits, including:

1. Strengthening cooperative structures for irrigation, farming, and agro-processing.
2. Providing economic opportunities for unemployed youth and thus reducing the phenomenon of rural exodus.
3. Improving access to markets through the establishment of new AHA plots (up from 5% in 2017) and enabling two to three agricultural campaigns per year, instead of one, on the new AHA plots, creating more opportunities for women farmers, particularly for women not previously exposed to such opportunities.
4. Including female-headed households.


### Economic co-benefits

The implementation of the project will create direct jobs in the construction of the plots, silting up and planting of windbreaks, fencing off deforested areas around the perimeters. These jobs will mainly involve local labour and there will also be new indirect jobs around the sites for operations such as irrigation, agro-processing, marketing, etc., which will result in the production of agricultural products.
Approximately 12,000 farmers will benefit, not counting the labour for clearing land, planting trees, and other activities. The project is expected to result in the development of agricultural marketing chains as well as product processing and conservation activities. Enhanced yields and new income streams will be combined with savings on diesel costs to help farmers pay off their loans from cooperatives with capacity to take loans to further expand production and processing facilities.


### Gender sensitivity and social inclusion

The FP acknowledges the highly constrained socio-economic position of women in rural Niger, and the high rate of youth under- and unemployment, and states that "In the selection


---
**Page 127**
---

direct beneficiaries, the gender criterion is included to give women, young people and adults the opportunity to participate fully in the project. Jola will be created for women in sowing, maintenance, harvesting, threshing, marketing etc., as well as men. About 50% of direct and indirect beneficiaries are women. The project aims at achieving gender parity by setting numerical targets to ensure women's participation in decision-making structures, and their access to resources. However, the plan does not take into account the specific socio-economic realities and the ambitious targets in the plan, that is not fully addressed by the design of the project. In addition, the plan does not consider the gender parity gap which is the highest in Africa: 7 children per woman and can reach 9 in regions such as Maradi and Zinder" but no mention is made of mechanisms to support women's participation, such as cooperative childcare or other forms of support.

26. On the challenge that "It is common in rural areas for women to speak little and/or to agree with the views and decisions of men", the plan suggests a rather vague solution: "To allow or even encourage women to express their opinions and ideas". To respond to a question from the participants on how to ensure women's participation, the project team presented two points of view during the various meetings related to the project. In response to a question from the participants about how to ensure women's participation in decision-making structures, empowerment and women's leadership in the project region, the AHA responded that "Since 2006, SOKO has been working with women in the project area (Agadez, Diffa, Maradi, Tchad, Zinder) to empower them. The project will continue this work". In response to a question from the participants about how to ensure women's participation in decision-making structures, empowerment and women's leadership in the project region, the AHA responded that "Since 2010, SOKO has been working with women in the project area (Agadez, Diffa, Maradi, Tchad, Zinder) to empower them. The project will continue this work". The Project can draw inspiration from it as an organisational mechanism in Outre 3.2, especially regarding the empowerment of women and their participation in decision-making structures.

14. Needs of the recipient Scale: High

Because of the demonstrably high vulnerability of the country to a range of external shocks including climate change, the development of beneficiary groups to climate resilience is critical for the overall income and development level of Niger. The needs of the recipient are rated as HIGH. Niger is a Landlocked Least Developed Country (LLDC) and is among the Low Income and Deficit Countries (LIDCs). It has a population of 21 million people, with an average age of 18 years old capital, and is ranked last in terms of the Human Development Index, with its economy highly dependent on agriculture. The country's vulnerability to climate change is high, and it was recently classified as vulnerable to multidimensional poverty, and this is likely to have increased in the last two years.

Footnote:
For example, Olaide Aderin (AHA) noted women's participation in her previous projects in Niger: lessons from Dimera Clubs. Gender & Development, 20(3), S55-S69. DOI: 10.1080/14649357.2012.713163


---
**Page 128**
---

29. Niger is also one of the hottest and driest countries in the world, with most of its area covered by the southward-encroaching Sahara Desert, and even the Sahel region of the country is subject to serious climate change threats. For rural populations for whom agriculture is the main source of income and livelihood, these changes are felt more acutely than anywhere else; pressure on natural resources such as water and grazing is also one of the underlying causes of conflict in the region.

30. The average temperature has increased by 0.35°C per decade, rainfall has been generally declining with decadal averages falling from an average of over 1,200 mm in the 1970s to less than 800 mm since the 1990s, with domestic agricultural production currently structurally in deficit.

31. Despite the ongoing effects of the Government of Niger and its financial and technical partners to improve food security and resilience against climate change, there is a need for increased resilience and food security; greater investment is needed. Niger's fiscal gap is a barrier to addressing financing needs. Niger has a per capita gross domestic product (GDP) of US$156. It had a significant increase in its GDP from 2019 to 2020, with an increase of over USD 1 billion in 2020, and has a B3 credit rating by Moody's. This suggests the country is just able to service foreign debt but not domestic debt. The project will be implemented through the World Bank/ROAD co-finance to the project, but relies also on grant finance for the bulk of the project.

### 1.5 Country ownership

#### Scale: High

32. Country ownership of the proposed project is rated as HIGH since it demonstrates strong alignment with national policies and strategies, and a high degree of engagement under the leadership of the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock (MoAL), the National Agency for Water Resources (ANAR), as well as a range of other public sector actors, and a growing engagement with the private sector. It is also supported by the Bureau International de la Recherche Agronomique pour le Développement (BIRAD) as the Accredited Entity for the project as a whole, and also as the Executing Entity for the GEF-funded agricultural loan facility under Output 3.1, building on the long established partnership between BIRAD and the MoAL.

33. The project is well aligned with all of Niger's key policies, strategies, plans and programmes, including the National Development Plan (NDP); the National Water Framework Agreement; the National Climate Change Adaptation Plan (FANA); the Sustainable Development Strategy and Inclusive Growth Vision 2035; the IN Initiative for Food and Nutrition Security and Sustainable Livelihoods; the National Agricultural Policy; the National Strategy in Niger; and the National Environmental Protection Policy. In particular, the project addresses the following national priorities: diversification of agricultural production (diversification and intensification of irrigated crops), mobilization of surface water and groundwater resources, promotion of integrated farming activities and the development of mutual aid associations.

34. The independent TAP notes that this project will contribute directly to the implementation of Niger's 2020 Four-year Contract Plan between the Office of Hydro-Agricultural Development (OHADHA) and the Niger State, promoting the goals that: (a) the production systems are diversified in order to ensure food security; (b) they are sustainable, productive and profitable; (c) the day-to-day management of AHAG is rational and efficient; (d) the project's results are monitored and evaluated on a regular basis so that they are functional and sustainable, as well as climate-resilient. This four-year contract-plan targets 4,008 hectares of new hydro-agricultural development (of which the project contributes 1,600), and 2,000 hectares of water mobilization (of which the project contributes 500). The project also aims at the mobilization of 8 water mobilization works. The independent TAP considers that this direct alignment with public commitments will ensure a high level of success throughout the project's implementation.


---
**Page 129**
---

The project was developed following a thorough stakeholder engagement process on the public side involving the NDA Agriculture Ministry (the Directorates of Agriculture, Livestock, Land tenure, Investment, Irrigation, OMAHA), the National Environmental Assessment Office, the Hydrology and Water Resources Department, the Federal Ministry of Environment, Energy and Finance, including the Directorate in charge of Microfinance Institutions. The National Agricultural Loan Facility (NALF) was established by the Government on the Agricultural Loan Facility, but full engagement of local financial institutions is still to take place, reflecting their limited interest or capacity.

At the local scale, consultations took place in 25 villages, 22 communities and 15 departments across the regions of Maradi, Tahoua, Agadez, Zinder and Diffa. Regional Government officials (the Directorates of Agriculture, Livestock, Land tenure, Irrigation, OMAHA), Environment, Hydrology and Water Resources were involved in these consultations. Farmers' groups were all consulted. Civil society and farmers’ organizations (mixed and women’s) actively contributed to the preparation of the project proposal and participated in the selection of beneficiary villages. During these consultations, vulnerable groups expressed their interest in and support for the project activities.

### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

**Scale: Medium to high**

The project's overall efficiency and effectiveness is rated at MEDIUM TO HIGH. The total project cost is N$240 million (US$35.7 million), of which N$89.5 million will be financed from the World Bank, which forms 66 per cent of the total cost. The remaining EUR 15.4 million, or 34 per cent of the total project cost, is being provided by the Government of Niger (N$20.7 million) and the European Union (EUR 9.8 million), and Senior Loan Finance from the AE RABD (EUR 9.24 million) which will support implementation of all the project components. The kind contributions from the Government of Niger are significant, especially in view of the fact that this is a large-scale project for which the project can be considered significant, given that Niger is an LDC with a limited revenue base.

As part of the project activities, 150 farmers' groups will benefit directly from blended finance from Niger's Local Financial Institutions. The national agriculture bank, BAGRI, has already expressed written interest in participating in the Agricultural Loan Facility. Through this facility, BAGRI will provide loans to smallholder farmers and other beneficiaries who will blend the GCF funds with their own funds to make loans for farming and agri-processing activities. This will be a win-win situation as it will allow BAGRI to become more involved in preservation, warehousing and processing to expand their operations. It could also provide finance for the purchase of inputs such as seeds, fertilizers and pesticides. Farmers involved in this technology such loans will build on the productivity gains stimulated through the project, and will be able to use the proceeds from these activities to invest in other small agriculture and produce value chains. The details of the loan facility have not been convincingly worked out so far, yet, leaving an element of uncertainty on it.

The project is expected to contribute significantly towards sustainable hydro-agricultural developments, with financing of this project requested through a GCF grant and a limited loan facility from BAGRI. This will help to reduce the level of poverty in the region and avoid increasing the level of indebtedness. This GCF investment will have several benefits for Niger in terms of (i) adaptation of the most vulnerable populations; (ii) mitigation efforts, improvement of water resources management, reduction of soil erosion, conservation of energy; and (iii) rational management of irrigation water. The independent TAP is pleased to use this project as a pilot for the implementation of the National Irrigation Management Action Plan (NIMAP) of the AHA (including plots, flood defences, irrigation and electrical equipment), through the Irrigation Water Users Associations in partnership with OMAHA. This includes

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel
