## The effectiveness of interventions will be ensured using best practice climate-smart agriculture (CSA) technologies and practices, in close collaboration with CGIAR (the Advisory Group for International Agricultural Research), including the CGIAR Research Program on Water, Land and Ecosystems (WLE). Implementation of these smart agriculture actions requires relatively little investment, and is cost-effective. The project will be implemented over a period of 10 years and its maintenance is relatively inexpensive. The profitability analysis of the developed irrigation system shows that the project will generate positive net present value in the short term, but it will bear to the costs of replacing irrigation systems with a lifespan of five to ten years. Output 3.2 of the project ensures the establishment of an integrated system for operation and maintenance of the irrigation infrastructure.

### The economic analysis conducted for the project, which aims to enable the farmers in the AHA to carry out three agriculture campaigns per year, uses a conservative scenario of two campaigns per year, and even under this scenario, the project is feasible. The project Internal Rate of Return (IRR) is 12%, with an NPV of EUR 54,113, which is positive. With the three-campaign scenario, the project economic rate of return increases to 18% with a positive NPV of EUR 90,765.

### The cost of the GCF investment is a modest EUR 2 per beneficiary, taking into account that it includes the costs of the pilot phase and scaling up through the project. The project's cost-effectiveness comes also from its cross-cutting nature - with on-the one hand, water efficiency and fuel switching contributing to climate change adaptation, and on the other hand, energy efficiency and fuel switching contributing to climate change mitigation.

### The GCF investment of EUR 22,905/67 of the GCF funds invested, are considered to be contributing to adaptation, with 24% of GCF funds invested in water efficiency and 76% in energy efficiency. If the cost of the GCF investment is taken into account, the estimated GCF cost per EUR saved, is EUR 32. This is a high cost and reflects the current reality of the high upfront cost of installing solar PV pumping systems compared to diesel pumps, which are still widely used with the fossil-fuel based alternatives that currently dominate African markets. At the same time, the independent technical advisory panel notes that there may be opportunities for scaling up feasible groundwater utilization in the region to adapt to climate change, since it will avoid locking in a new generation of diesel pumps, which have an average lifespan of 20-25 years, which would be in contradiction with Niger's NDC commitments.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### This is an excellent project which meets all of the GCF's investment criteria. In order to ensure that it fulfills its potential in terms of achieving a paradigm shift through crowding in private finance and scaling up, it will be necessary for the project to be aligned with the Agricultural Loan Facility. An in-depth market and design study is needed, to ensure that the facility and any associated guarantee funds are based on a feasible financial model, including consideration of the cost of scaling up the project. The independent technical advisory panel recommends this funding proposal for approval, subject to the following condition:


---

---

**(a)** Prior to the disbursement of funds for Output 3.2 of the Funded Activity, the accredited entity shall finalize and submit to the Fund an additional report, in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat containing:

- (i) an in-depth market and design study that [E] assesses the financial viability of the loan facility, including consideration of likely default rates, and
- (ii) proposes a design for the Facility that can maximize potential both to crowd in private sector investment and to mitigate risks associated with climate change.

**(b)** If the market study confirms the potential for the loan facility, then prior to the disbursement of funds for Output 3.2 of the Funded Activity, the AIE should provide, in form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat:

- (i) a detailed technical assistance programme targeting both lenders and borrowers
to ensure successful implementation of the loan component.

# Introduction

This assessment aims to provide information and data on the gender situation in the design and implementation of the "High-Growth Agricultural Development Project with Climate Resilient Smallholder Agriculture Prediction" in Niger.

The project is structured around 3 components: i) Sustainable development and rehabilitation of permanent crops; ii) Mitigation of change effects of climate change; iii) Support for the sustainable increase of agricultural productivity and farmers' income in irrigated areas on developed and rehabilitated permanent crops.

The gender assessment analyzes gender inequalities in several areas and identifies opportunities that could be seized through project activities to close the gaps that exist within beneficiary communities. The identification of these opportunities is based on a review of existing literature, which will be supplemented with commune- and site-specific information and data prior to project implementation. This document provides guidance for the development and implementation of a Gender Action Plan that aims more specifically to:

o Improve the participation of women, youth and disadvantaged groups in decision-making and planning processes;

o Improve the accessibility of women, youth and disadvantaged groups to the learning and training opportunities provided by the project;

o Promote information and communication to integrate gender throughout the process;

o Promote gender awareness among project staff, partners and other focal points.

## 1. Gender mainstreaming in Niger national institutional framework

### 1.1 National initiatives

In Niger at the central level, the Ministry for the Promotion of Women and Child Protection (MPPFE) is the body responsible for implementing actions for the promotion of women and gender equality. It includes a Directorate for the Promotion of Women and Gender which ensures, from an operational point of view, the implementation of the national policy on gender equality in all areas of action of women and gender. The Ministry also has decentralized services that ensure the coordination, monitoring and evaluation of gender mainstreaming.

At regional level, there are decentralized communities and municipalities which are directly involved in the implementation of PNG through their Development Plans. So, they work in close collaboration with the various regional Ministries in charge of Gender in order to ensure that gender is taken into account in all interventions.

In 2018, the Government of Niger has adopted a National Gender Policy (PNG), the overall objective of which is to promote and support the full participation of women and men in the achievement of equity and equality of opportunity and citizenship. This PNG has five strategic axes:

- Strengthening the role of women and men as actors for more equality between men and women;
- Strengthening of the political and legal framework favorable to the effective enjoyment of the rights of women and girls, to combat gender-based violence and to ensure equal participation of men and women in the management of power;
- Economic empowerment of women and men through sustainable management of the environment, disaster risk management, migration and humanitarian emergencies;
- Strengthening of institutional mechanisms and organizational frameworks for coordination, monitoring-evaluation and partnership.


---
**Page 137**
---

In addition to this ministry, there are other institutional mechanisms contributing to the achievement of the same objective, notably: i) the Directorate for the Enrolment of Girls, which is the body responsible for implementing the government's policy on the enrolment of girls; ii) the Gender Advisers to the President and the Prime Minister who provide technical advice on gender equality in the country’s development policies and programs; iii) the National Observatory on Gender Equality (ONEG), which monitors progress towards the promotion of gender equality in the country’s development policies and programs, (iv) the National Observatory on Child Protection (ONEF), which monitors progress toward the protection of children's rights. The ONEG was established by decree No 2017-348 of August 9, 2017, is the monitoring mechanism for the promotion of gender equality and equity. The Observatory has a steering committee composed of representatives from various ministries and agencies that feed into reports and assessments; iv) gender cells and focal points within sectoral ministries. The general objective of these mechanisms is to ensure the systematic inclusion of gender in all activities of the Ministry for the Advancement of Women and Child Protection. The role of the cells is to monitor the systematic inclusion of gender in the development policies, programs and projects of their ministry. Other institutions that contribute to the promotion of gender equality are: i) the National Commission on Human Rights and Fundamental Freedoms which support advocacy for the creation of an environment conducive to the promotion of gender; civil society organizations and development partners, are also part of the system.


### 2.2. Policy and legal framework to reduce Gender Inequalities

At policy level, there is a Gender National Policy (2008), along with a sectoral plan (2009-2018) for the implementation of this policy. Moreover, some sectoral policies also include the promotion of gender equality as one of their objectives. The Constitution of Niger provides that no law can be enacted regardless of gender and prohibits sex-based discrimination. Niger has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women - CEDAW in 1999 and has signed the Optional Protocol to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women which enter into force in 2002; defines a minimal threshold for the participation to the management of public affairs. The National Action Plan (NAP) for the implementation of CEDAW, adopted by Niger, contributes to more gender equality.

Addressing some of the governance challenges in the public and private sector may bring particular benefits to women. For instance, improving the enabling business environment is likely to be particularly important for women, who tend to have less access to the money, time, literacy and social networks needed to start a business or expand an existing one. In addition, providing training and support funded by development partners could be considered. Efforts to enable the informal sector will also benefit women, as they are more likely than men to work in this sector. However, these efforts should be conducted in a sensitive manner, efforts to improve tenure security can also be especially beneficial for women by improving their access to land and other resources.

There are additional barriers for women trying to conduct business. For instance, the Civil Code provides for the man as the head of the household with 'marital authority' over his wife and explicitly limits her legal capacity in marriage, including with respect to exercising a profession and opening a bank account. Even though the Commercial Code allows women to be independent.


### 2. Overview of Gender inequalities in Niger

#### 2.1. Gender analysis in demographic level

As of July 2018, Niger's population was estimated at 27.4 million inhabitants; it is expected to reach 34.5 million in 2030 and more than triple in 2050 to 69 million. Women account for less than half of the population (52.4%), and this share has actually increased in the last seven years according to data from the National Statistical Institute. They accounted for just over half (53.7%) of the total population


---
**Page 138**
---

In 2012, Women’s fertility rate is one of the highest in Africa, 7.6 children per woman and can reach 9 in regions such as Maradi and Zinder. The high fertility trends, combined with a rapid decline in child mortality, contribute significantly to the very high rate of population growth of 3.9 percent. Some cultural factors are also responsible for this high fertility rate. In addition, there is a lack of income rises in the face of poverty, and gender discrimination. Without improvements in these general conditions, it will be difficult to reduce fertility rates. However, if women have access to active (media) engagement to discuss and challenge established norms, population growth is likely to reduce by 2015. The government has already taken steps towards this goal through the media to develop their well-being (better education, health and nutritional status, employment and income generation). This will help to reduce fertility rates.

Niger was ranked 189 out of 189 countries in the 2017 Human Development Index (HDI), with a value of 0.356, which is 0.01 points higher than its HDI in 2016, corresponding to an increase of 6.85 percent. Based on the sex-disaggregated data, the Gender Development index (GDI) is calculated as the ratio of the female to the male HDI for these gender inequalities: education (measured by female and male expected years of schooling), health (measured by life expectancy at birth), education (measured by female and male expected years of schooling for children aged 15-24), and income (measured by female and male estimated GNI per capita). In 2017, the female HDI was 0.317 in Niger while the male HDI was 0.689.

Furthermore, a Gender Inequality Index (GII) is calculated to reflect gender-based inequalities in three dimensions: reproductive health, empowerment and the labour market. The GII is calculated as the sum of the values for each dimension (measured by the share of parliamentary seats held by women and attainment in secondary and higher education), minus the maximum value possible for that dimension. The maternal mortality rate for women and men; Niger is ranked 154 out of 189 countries according to UN Annual report, with a GII value of 0.630. The maternal mortality ratio (MR) per 100,000 live births in 2017 was 1022 for women and 1025 for men; the share of parliamentary seats held by women out of all 177, representing an increase of 16.3 percent. However, the number of women parliamentary representatives is still very low: only one woman in a total of 89 representatives (1.14 percent). Regarding representation in Government, the share of women is only 18%, which is far from the average for sub-Saharan Africa countries, where it reaches 25%. The share of women out of a total of 3752, corresponding to a participation rate of 16%, which is much lower in Zinder. The female literacy rate was 40% compared with 89% for men; the female secondary level of education came up to 6.5 percent of their male counterparts. The participation of women in the labour market reached a higher level of gender inequalities with 27.5 percent, compared to male participation of 90.7 percent.

As for the Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI), Niger ranks 133rd out of 159 with a SIGI value of 0.4845, which is 0.06 points higher than its SIGI in 2016, corresponding to an increase of 13.7 percent. The index measures gender discrimination against women in social institutions (formal and informal laws, social norms, and practices) that perpetuate gender inequalities. Niger has the highest level of gender discrimination among African countries with a high level of gender discrimination based on the SIGI. The table 3 below summarizes the above-mentioned criteria.

Gender inequalities contribute to poverty which disproportionately affects women, girls and children. Given the high levels of gender inequality, it is not surprising that Niger is one of the poorest countries in the World with a poverty rate of 44.1 percent. Poverty is more important in rural than urban areas. The GII for Niger is 0.65 which is higher than the average GII for Sub-Saharan Africa reflected with the Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI) for which Niger had a value of 0.055 in 2012, and also higher than the MPI for rural areas in Niger which was 0.087. The GII is higher between urban and rural areas regarding MPI ratio (areas 0.696) which is more than two times higher compared to the national average of 0.354. This shows that gender inequalities are a major factor in Zinder (0541) and Dosso (0520). High poverty level in rural areas and its effects on people's vulnerability gives evidence on the need to create enabling environment for financing climate resilient agriculture.

### Specific gender issues according to the relevant sectors in the project

Social norms and expectations limit the full potential economic of women, and by implication of society as a whole. This sector is critical for economic growth in the poorest country which relies heavily on women. Small and medium enterprises (SMEs) are an important source of employment and income generation for businesses; they have uneven financial needs and face various barriers to access to assets and financing. Access to credit can open up economic opportunities for rural Nepalese women, and bank accounts can be a means of saving money. However, women entrepreneurs face greater challenges than men in gaining access to financial services. Access to land is also an important issue as it is estimated that 70 percent of the agricultural labor force are women. Key barriers to accessing to financing are gender based social, cultural and legal. To transform the situation under a changing climate, the Government of Nepal needs to close the Gender Gap in agriculture.

### Employment

About 34 percent of women out of the labor force are employed as opposed to 51 percent for men. On average, active employment is about 20 fewer hours compared to men (69 for women and 89 for men) while receiving lower earnings. In terms of gross national income, it is estimated at US$ 481 per year

# 2.2.3. Land access

Whereas women are less productive and access to land through inheritance is the dominant mode and is generally reserved for men (39% of women own land in 2012). But access to land ownership is possible under the new approach, which allows women to register their names as co-owners. The rate of women as plot owner is low. Many of whom are generally working as spouses. The new national approach on ARMA is that women and men have the same right and the equal access to land.

With the AHAAL and in this project, land will be voluntarily handed over to ONHAA by landowners in a regionally defined area. Each plot will be 1 ha (10,000 m²) or smaller. The plots will be divided into 0.125 ha plots. The plot units will be allocated either to a farmer (male, female, young), or to a husband and its members (headed by a man or a woman). Plot allocations will be made on the basis of a family unit. In case of polygamous unions, women are allowed to have their own plots if they wish.

With the AHAAL and in this project, land will be voluntarily handed over to ONHAA by landowners in a regionally defined area. Each plot will be 1 ha (10,000 m²) or smaller. The plots will be divided into 0.125 ha plots. The plot units will be allocated either to a farmer (male, female, young), or to a husband and its members (headed by a man or a woman). Plot allocations will be made on the basis of a family unit. In case of polygamous unions, women are allowed to have their own plots if they wish.

The **Hydro agricultural areas** under ONHAA management is the access equality between men and women.

To improve the achievement of project results, sensitization, information and capacity building sessions are planned during the developing of the project implementation, for men and women. This will facilitate the participation in projects and activities that are being implemented by the project process. Finally, it is important to note that the redistribution of plots follows an administrative procedure which is carried out by the ONHAA management team.

This practice is aligned to axis 3 of the National Gender Policy (Economic empowerment and inclusive growth). The method of distribution is not influenced by traditional practices or customs.

Also, the traditional system of land management is not favorable to individual property rights. In the new form of redistribution of the project, land belong to the state through ONHAA. For individuals, it concerns a right of usufruct for a period of 30 years. This usufruct is subject to a formal contract signed between ONHAA and each beneficiary. So, this form of access does not meet with resistance from traditional authorities.

In any case, the formation and the approval of an Environmental and Social Impact assessment (ESIA) is planned for each subject before the beginning of the field works. ### 2.2.4. Education

In rural areas, the literacy rate of women aged 15 years old and higher is very low, particularly in rural areas where it was 11 percent in 2014/15. Long standing traditions attach little value to the education of girls than of boys demonstrated by a high propensity of literate women compared to men, with respective rates of literacy of 73% for women and 86% for men.

The primary Gross Enrolment Ratio (GER) in primary school is 90.5 percent for both boys and girls. The secondary GER is 41.2 percent for boys and 35.5 percent for girls, with a significant gender gap of 5.7 percentage points. In the tertiary sector, the GER is 68.5 percent for men compared to 50.9 percent for women.

2.4. Common gender beliefs, perceptions and stereotypes

At the customary level, in general, there are social norms that formally or informally regulate the respective roles of women and men. Findings from a World Bank study in this area (World Bank, 2016) reveal that these norms are not only widely prevalent but also deeply rooted in society. These norms are core cultural values of a group or society and shape everyday conduct in private and public life. These norms are often referred to as gender roles, which define the expectations placed on men and women regarding their behavior, activities, and responsibilities within families and communities.

These norms have the effect of limiting women's access to agricultural resources by stating that it is socially unacceptable for women to inherit land or engage in agricultural activities.

The law also plays a role in defining the rights and obligations of men and women. For example, the price they are paid for their labor is often lower than what men receive. Women are strongly criticized for breaking customs and even accused of being a degree to their families. According to customary law, women are not considered as decision makers. As for women, they depend on them and must be under the protection of men.

In customary law, property rights and rules of intergenerational transmission are still governed by gender roles. In many cases, these norms are not codified in written laws but are attributed to social and customary norms, even though some norms are often unfairly applied or lack a genuine legal basis.

In terms of beliefs, the two main religions are Islam and Christianity; the first conceives 98% of the population and the second about 1%. Beyond spiritual beliefs, religions are practices that condition existence. They can be seen as a way to explain life's mysteries, such as birth, death, illness or children between spouses. In this configuration, customs and traditions are intertwined with certain rules of life dictated by religion.

3. Vulnerability to climate change

Analysis of vulnerability to food insecurity by gender of household head in rural areas shows that people living in female-headed households are more vulnerable to food insecurity related to the adverse effects of climate change. Female-headed households are more food insecure than male-headed households. There are 8.7% of female-headed households versus 2.7% of male-headed

# 4. Information and communication

Gender inequalities in the agricultural sector are not only limited to access to land, but also to the personnel who are supposed to conduct policies, transmit the message and supervise the agricultural population. This is particularly true for women, who are often excluded from decision-making processes.

A communication strategy, aimed at both men and women beneficiaries, will be implemented at the local level. This process aims to improve their understanding of the adverse effects of climate change and to promote the adoption of appropriate adaptation measures. The strategy includes the implementation of appropriate technical and technological innovations to adapt to the adverse effects of climate change.

# 5. Issues related to women's participation in the project

The many disparities that exist between men and women, especially in the areas of education, vulnerability or decision-making power over resources (especially land), decision-making, etc., represent constraints that can limit the full participation of women in project activities. Women face a number of obstacles when it comes to accessing agricultural inputs, credit services and other services and facilities offered by the project (training, capacity building, access to inputs, etc.).

The following measures will be implemented during the various stages of project implementation to facilitate and encourage women's participation in the various project activities.

| Women's Vulnerabilities | Risks to the project | Mitigation measures |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Low level of understanding of the adverse effects of climate change can lead to a low level of ownership of the adaptation measures at the collective level. | Use local languages in awareness campaigns for project beneficiaries. |
| Poverty/Low income | Difficulties for women to pay land lease fees, as well as the maintenance costs of the waterworks services. | Prioritize women's agricultural credit applications. |
| Difficulties or impossibility of using harvesters for sowing or watering the fields | The project will be carried out within a secure land framework where all the land to be cultivated is under the control of CNHHA, which will sign an operating contract with each beneficiary (man, woman, landowner). |

# Summary of Gender Action Plan

## Overview of the Gender Action Plan

### General Objective

Enhance men and women’s equal participation in the project through the improvement and diversification of agricultural production (including 50% women and girls) in rural communities currently dependent on rain-fed agriculture or a regime of higher

### Gender Mainstreaming Results

#### Component 1: Sustainable Development and Rehabilitation of Areas Vulnerable to the Negative Effects of Climate Change and Reducing GHG Emissions

R1. The capacities of vulnerable groups including women in terms of gender mainstreaming in the project are strengthened.

- Train women and young producers (farmers) regarding agricultural techniques that are resilient to climate change.
- Train women and young people in associative life in order to be elected within the governing structure of cooperatives and Water Users Associations (AUE).
- Strengthen the capacities of women and young people in associative and organizational life.

R2. The technical and organizational capacities of vulnerable actors including 50% of women are strengthened.

- Build the capacities of actors from the technical support services of the Ministries involved in the gender equality project to meet the differentiated needs of men, women and young people.
- Train women and young people in interpreting agro-meteorological data.
- Strengthen the capacity of women and young women of local communities to include smart agriculture into their activities.

R3. Women and men have equal access to decision-making.

- Train women and young people in the use of tools for monitoring changes in natural resources.
- Strengthen the capacities of women and men and young people in the use of Tools for monitoring changes in natural resources.

#### Component 2: Support of the Sustainable Increase in Agricultural Productivity and Income of Farmers’ Groups in Areas Vulnerable to Climate Change and Rehabilitated Arid/Desertified Areas

R3. Women and men have equal access to decision-making.

- Make women and young people participants in decision-making relating to project activities.
- Choose competent women as members of the teams responsible for the land registration process.
- Build women, men, and young people’s capacity to improve the functionality of their organizations and associations (cooperatives and Water Users Associations).
- Facilitate the access of women heads of households to land and agricultural technologies.

R4. Women and men have equal access to agricultural and climate-resilient technologies

- Facilitate the access of women heads of household to land and agricultural technologies

### Introduction to the Gender Action Plan Logframe

The logical framework below summarizes the objectives, the results to be achieved in order to ensure the integration of equality and gender equity in all the components of the project.

For all activities, baseline data on the number of women/men will be more precise at the implementation level. This will allow for a better identification of women’s needs and a more accurate gender analysis will be carried out in each project intervention site. The aim is to identify women heads of households who are vulnerable or marginalized due to their gender and to provide them with specific training on climate change adaptation. In addition, a gender budgeting analysis will be carried out in order to be able to provide training to the appropriate administration in order to train women and young people particularly.

Project Impact : The resilience of vulnerable farming communities, including 50% of women and girls, to the negative impacts of climate change through (i) poverty reduction and strengthening food security through increased agricultural production, and (ii) restoring and maintaining soil fertility through innovative and climate-resilient agricultural technology

Main Expected Results with Gender Mainstreaming:
R1: The capacity of vulnerable groups including women in terms of gender mainstreaming in the project is enhanced
R2: The capacities of actors involved in the project including 50% of different needs of men and women are strengthened
R3: Women and men have equal access to decision-making bodies
R4: Men and women have equal access to climate-resilient technologies
R5: Gender equity in the development of stakeholder financial capacities for the climate-resilient agricultural practices promotion is ensured
R6: The capacity to identify best gender experiences and practices of the project is disseminated

The case studies, lessons learned, and best gender experiences and practices of the project are disseminated
